Compressed Permutation Oracles
Joseph Carolan

Abstract
The analysis of quantum algorithms which query random, invertible permutations has been a long-standing challenge in cryptography. Many techniques which apply to random oracles fail, or are not known to generalize to this setting. As a result, foundational cryptographic constructions involving permutations often lack quantum security proofs. With the aim of closing this gap, we develop and prove soundness of a compressed permutation oracle. Our construction shares many of the attractive features of Zhandry’s original compressed function oracle: the purification is a small list of input-output pairs which meaningfully reflect an algorithm’s knowledge of the oracle. We then apply this framework to show that the Feistel construction with seven rounds is a strong quantum PRP, resolving an open question of (Zhandry, 2012). We further re-prove essentially all known quantum query lower bounds in the random permutation model, notably the collision and preimage resistance of both Sponge and Davies-Meyer, hardness of double-sided zero search and sparse predicate search, and give new lower bounds for cycle finding and the one-more problem.



prob]Problem
conj]Conjecture

1
Introduction

Quantum algorithms which query random permutations and their inverse are ubiquitous in quantum and post-quantum cryptography
[
Zha16
,
Ros22
,
Zha21
,
Coj+25
,
Ala+23
,
Ala+25
]
. This model is commonly referred to as the quantum random permutation model, or QRPM. Like the related quantum random oracle model (the QROM), this model is an idealization. Where the QROM is an idealization of hash functions, the QRPM is an idealization of cryptographic permutations and ciphers.

Many tools exist for analyzing the QROM
[
Zha12
,
Bon+11
,
Unr14
,
YZ21
]
. One of the most powerful is the compressed oracle
[
Zha19
]
, which analyzes the purification of a random oracle in a specially chosen basis that has an intuitive interpretation. In particular, the purification can be written as a
database
of input-output pairs which meaningfully correspond to points the adversary has knowledge of. Moreover, each query adds at most a single such pair to the database. The introduction of this tool led to a slew of new results in quantum and post-quantum cryptography
[
Don+21
,
Chu+21
]
, as well as query complexity
[
LZ19
,
HM23
]
.

The QRPM has proven more challenging to analyze. For instance, the compressed oracle technique has resisted many attempts at generalizations to permutation oracles
[
Unr21
,
Unr23
,
Ros22
,
Cza+19
]
—or any correlated oracle distribution.
Even basic search problems remained open until recently
[
CP24
,
HY18
,
Zha21
,
Unr21
,
MMW24
]
, and despite recent progress on permutation based hash functions
[
CPZ24
,
Ala+25
,
Coj+25
]
, many important constructions still lack quantum security proofs.

An emblematic example is the Feistel cipher, also known as the Luby-Rackoff cipher or Feistel network, which is a standard construction of a psuedorandom permutation from a psuedorandom function
[
LR88
]
. A number of influential block ciphers, such as DES
[
Nat77
]
and Blowfish
[
Sch93
]
, are based on this construction.
For purposes of illustration, suppose that
h,k,f
are pseudorandom functions from
n
bits to
n
bits. Then, the Feistel network constructs a
2n
bit permutation
\varphi
as the following sequence of operations. To compute
\varphi(x)
:

1.

Take the first
n
bits of
x
, referred to as
x_{L}
, and compute
h(x_{L})
. XOR this value into the last
n
bits of
x
, referred to as
x_{R}
, obtaining
x^{\prime}=x_{L}\|(x_{R}\oplus h(x_{L}))
.

2.

Take the last
n
bits of
x^{\prime}
, and compute
k(x_{R}^{\prime})
. XOR the result into
x^{\prime}_{L}
, obtaining
x^{\prime\prime}=(x_{L}^{\prime}\oplus k(x_{R}^{\prime}))\|x_{R}^{\prime}
.

3.

Take the first
n
bits of
x^{\prime\prime}
, and compute
f(x_{L}^{\prime\prime})
. XOR the result into
x^{\prime\prime}_{R}
, obtaining
y=x_{L}^{\prime\prime}\|(x_{R}^{\prime\prime}\oplus f(x_{L}^{\prime\prime}))
, where
y=\varphi(x)
.

Figure 1:
An illustration of three round Feistel.

This procedure is depicted in
Figure

1
. The three functions corresponds to three rounds, and the construction can be extended with more functions in alternating direction for any number of rounds. It is known that the three round construction is indistinguishable from a random permutation with only forwards classical queries, and that four rounds are indistinguishable with forwards and inverse classical queries
[
LR88
]
. With quantum queries—the strongest plausible attack model—essentially nothing is known about security. This question was first raised by Zhandry in 2012
[
Zha12
]
, yet remains beyond existing techniques.
1
1

1

For instance, in a 2025 revision to
[
Zha16
]
, Zhandry says “The quantum security of Feistel networks remains a largely open, very challenging problem.”

Hosoyamada and Iwata
[
HI19
]
claimed a proof that four round Feistel is a quantum psuedorandom permutation or qPRP
2
2

2

Without the qualifier of strong, this means forwards-only secure.
, but their proof was found to contain a bug
[
Bha+24
]
. The same paper which found the bug proved security against non-adaptive, forwards only queries.
On the algorithmic side, it is known that there is a quantum distinguishing attack for three rounds with forwards only queries
[
KM10
]
, and four rounds with both forwards and backwards queries
[
Ito+19
]
. These results indicate that classical proof techniques for Feistel security must break down.

1.1
Our results

Our main result is a compressed oracle technique for analyzing two-way accessible permutations. Our construction is simple to apply, and shares many features of Zhandry’s original compressed function oracle
[
Zha19
]
. In particular, our construction has a purifying register which is a list of input-output pairs, referred to as the compressed database. We establish that the construction is

1.

Sound
, in that it faithfully represents algorithms querying two-way accessible random permutations.

2.

Bounded
, in that the size of the compressed database grows by at most one with each query.

3.

Meaningful
, in that the points defined by the database correspond to points the algorithm “knows”.

We defer to
Section

3
for a formal description of both the construction and the above properties—note that the construction itself is only a few pages.
At a high level, the construction is similar to the compressed function oracle but with two changes. First, the compression operator explicitly maintains injectivity of the database. Second, to answer inverse queries, we invert the database before and after answering.

The idea of inverting the truth table to answer inverse queries is due to Unruh
[
Unr23
]
; our construction is a small modification to Unruh’s proposal.
3
3

3

In
[
Unr23
]
, however, soundness is only conjectured.
The proof that this simple construction is sound constitutes the main technical component of this work.

Theorem

(Informal version of
Theorem

5.19
)
.

No algorithm can distinguish the compressed permutation oracle described above from a truly random size
N
permutation unless it makes
\Omega(\sqrt[12]{N})
bidirectional quantum queries.

While our bound is exponential in the size of an input to the permutation, it does not seem tight.
Once the construction is in place, we apply it to a number of problems in the QRPM. The main application is a proof that the seven round Feistel construction is a strong quantum PRP.

Theorem

(Informal version of
Theorem

6.5
)
.

No algorithm can distinguish the seven round Feistel construction from a truly random size
N
permutation unless it makes
\Omega(\sqrt[12]{N})
bidirectional quantum queries.

Additionally, we give a generic quantum lower bound for any predicate search problem in the QRPM in terms of the sparsity of the predicate. We defer to
Section

7
for a formal statement, but note that this result is analogous to search lower bounds proved in the QROM by standard compressed oracle methods
[
Zha19
,
Chu+21
]
. Given our loose soundness bound, the result is not always tight.

However, essentially all known search lower bounds in the QRPM
[
HY18
,
Coj+25
,
CP24
,
CPZ24
,
Ala+25
,
MMW24
]
can be seen as a special case of this theorem. We re-prove the preimage and collision resistance of the sponge hash function
[
Ber+07
,
Ber+11a
,
Ber+11
]
, which underlies the current international standard SHA3, and the Davies-Meyer construction
[
Win84
]
, which underlies SHA1, SHA2, and MD5. Previous proofs are due to
[
Ala+25
]
and
[
Coj+25
]
, both of which required the development of new techniques. For the sponge, our bound is tighter than the one in
[
Ala+25
]
. We also prove the first lower bounds for the one-more problem and cycle finding in the QRPM, both of which appear—to the author’s knowledge—beyond the scope of existing techniques.

Intriguingly, we observe that the looseness in our bounds is entirely due to the soundness error of our construction. If the soundness analysis were improved, then all of the previously mentioned lower bounds would be tight.
4
4

4

With the possible exception of the bound for the one-more and cycle finding problem.
This suggests that our compressed permutation oracle accurately captures the difficulty of these problems.

Given the range of applications and simplicity of our construction, we expect it to be useful for many other problems in the QRPM.

1.2
Technical summary

To explain the intuition underlying our security proof, let us begin by considering an approach which does not appear to work. A natural starting point for analyzing quantum algorithms which query invertible permutations is to purify the permutation oracle, mirroring the approach of Zhandry for the compressed function oracle. If we represent a permutation/function from
[N]
to
[N]
by its truth table, we can contrast the two purifications:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\dots,y_{N},\\
y_{i}\neq y_{j}\end{subarray}}\ket{y_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\ket{y_{N}}}_{\text{permutation}},

vs.

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{N^{N}}}\sum_{y_{1},\dots,y_{N}}\ket{y_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\ket{y_{N}}}_{\text{function}}.

(1)

The two states look quite similar, but there is a subtle difference. The purification of random functions, in this representation, is a product state: each entry of the truth table is unentangled, reflecting the fact that the oracle outputs are uncorrelated. Then, a simple indicator that a query algorithm has learned about, say,
y_{i}
is to check whether the
i
-th register above remains in uniform superposition (no knowledge), or is entangled with an external register (knowledge gained). This observation is what enables standard compressed oracles.

In the permutation case, there is weak entanglement between registers
i
and
j
, reflecting the condition that
y_{i}\neq y_{j}
. While this might appear like a minor difference, it turns out that the intuition above completely breaks. In particular, the
i
-th register in the purification of a permutation is
maximally
entangled with the remaining registers. For every
j\neq i
,
y_{j}
eliminates one of the
[N]
possibilities for
y_{i}
. Taken together, the complement of
y_{i}
suffices to fix
y_{i}
. To check if
y_{i}
is queried, we must do more than check if register
i
is entangled, because it is maximally entangled before any queries are made.

To get around this, we must devise some other way to understand the entanglement between a query algorithm and the purification. One approach taken by Unruh
[
Unr21
]
and Rosmanis
[
Ros22
]
is to identify an injective database (a.k.a. an injective partial function)
I
with the sum of permutations agreeing with
I
on all well-defined points. Unfortunately, this leads to compressed database states which are non-orthogonal. This significantly complicates the analysis, especially in the case of inverse queries, limiting the scope of applications to simple search problems.

We will take a different approach based on expanding the purifying register. The point of this expansion is that we will be able to define
orthogonal
database states, which nonetheless characterize entanglement between the algorithm and oracle in an intuitive way. In more detail, we will write the full permutation
\varphi
as a three round Feistel network defined by functions labeled
h,k,f
, sandwiched on either side by random permutations
\pi
and
\omega
. We refer to this ensemble as
masked Feistel
, and it is depicted in
Figure

2
.

Figure 2:
The masked Feistel decomposition.

We can write the purification of this ensemble, using
N=4^{n}
as the permutation domain size, as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{(N!)^{2}(2^{n})^{3\cdot 2^{n}}}}\sum_{\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{S}_{N},h,k,f\in\mathbf{F}_{\sqrt{N},\sqrt{N}}}\ket{\pi,\omega,h,k,f}

(2)

where
\mathbf{F}_{\sqrt{N},\sqrt{N}}
is the set of functions from
\{0,1\}^{n}
to
\{0,1\}^{n}
and
\mathbf{S}_{N}
is the set of permutations on
\{0,1\}^{2n}
. We denote the permutation defined by this whole ensemble as
\varphi
. This
\varphi
is a product of a uniform random permutation (say
\pi
) and some independent permutation, and is therefore uniform random. At this point, it may appear that we have made backwards progress, given the complexity of this purification. However, let us ignore for a moment the outer
\pi,\omega
permutations, and consider what it means for
\varphi
to satisfy
\varphi(x)=y
. This fixes three input-output pairs in the
h,k,f
functions, corresponding to the three values reached along the evaluation of
\varphi(x)
—we call these three pairs a chain. In other words, input-output properties of
\varphi
correspond in a direct way to input-output properties of the random functions
h,k,f
.

Now, we apply the standard compressed oracle theory to analyze
h,k,f
. In this picture, the purifying registers for these functions are databases which begin empty. Each new query to the ensemble adds at most one new input-output pair to these databases, which we will use to reconstruct the input-output pairs of
\varphi
. Then, so long as each chain has no collisions (i.e. the same function input-output pair appearing in multiple chains), we can uniquely recover an injective database.

Of course, an adversarial algorithm could easily thwart this plan, by exploiting the periodicity of the three-round Feistel to distinguish it from a random permutation; or even making two queries that collide on the first
n
bits. This latter problem is the “trivialization of norm” faced by prior attempts to analyze quantum security of Feistel
[
HI19
,
Bha+24
]
.
The purpose of the twirling permutations
\pi
and
\omega
is to prevent the algorithm from detecting this internal structure. We do not compress these permutation, instead keeping them in uniform superposition. Intuitively, with high probability over the twirl, every new query will lead to a distinct first
n
bits on the internal Feistel. In turn, with high probability over the selection of the round function values, this will lead to a new chain (i.e. sequence of database values defining an input-output pair of
\varphi
) distinct from prior chains. This intuition can be formalized using the standard compressed oracle theory. At a high level, we define an isometry
\mathcal{I}
which maps a superposition over tuples
\pi,\omega,D_{h},D_{k},D_{f}
, where
D_{h},D_{k},D_{f}
correspond to function databases, to an injective database
I
. This isometry essentially acts as
5
5

5

The exact definition of an internal collision can be found in
Section

4
, and of the isometry can be found in
Section

5.1
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega,D_{h},D_{k},D_{f},\\
\text{supporting only $I$,}\\
\text{no internal collisions}\end{subarray}}\ket{\pi,\omega,D_{h},D_{k},D_{f}}\right)

\displaystyle=\ket{I},

(3)

where supporting
I
means the databases contain information to fix only the input-output pairs in
I
(and none else), and
K
is some normalization factor. The key point is that the states on the left hand side are mutually orthogonal, as they are supported on distinct sets of computational basis states.

Then, we show that this isometry
intertwines
the masked Feistel oracle with our compressed permutation oracle. In other words, if
\mathsf{cmfO}
is the compressed masked Feistel oracle and
\mathsf{cpO}
is the compressed permutation oracle, we show
\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}\approx\mathsf{cpO}\cdot\mathcal{I}
. Applying this across each query, we show that the final purified permutation (represented here as the tuple
\pi,\omega,h,k,f
) is mapped by
\mathcal{I}
to the purification in the compressed permutation oracle experiment (a partial injective database). Then, by Uhlmann’s theorem, the mixed state the algorithm sees after tracing out the purifying register is close in trace distance.

We stress that the masked Feistel ensemble is necessary only as a tool for proving soundness, and is not necessary to understand and apply the construction. Indeed, our applications to search lower bounds make no mention of masked Feistel, instead following essentially the same blueprint as compressed function oracle lower bounds. On the other hand, our proof that the seven round Feistel construction is a strong qPRP is quite similar. In particular, we can consider the seven round Feistel to be an instance of masked (three round) Feistel, where the twirling
\pi,\omega
are drawn from two round Feistel and two round inverse Feistel respectively. We show that this distribution
also
has the requisite statistical properties to hide the internal three rounds, allowing us to reuse many techniques from the soundness proof.

1.3
Related work

Prior work on constructing compressed permutation oracles has been met with limited success. Constructions were put forth by Unruh
[
Unr21
]
and Czajkowski et al
[
Cza+19
]
, though both were later found to be incorrect. A notable exception is the recent work of Majenz, Malavolta and Walter
[
MMW24
]
, referred to as the superposition permutation oracle. This construction was successfully employed to show search hardness for sparse relations, and is based on compressed oracle ideas. Unfortunately, this technique differs from standard compressed oracles in that the purification is not a small list of input-output pairs. This added complexity has limited the application of this technique to simple search problems involving only a single input-output pair—in contrast, our search lower bounds apply for predicates on any number of input-output pairs.

In the case where inverse queries are not allowed, a proposal by Rosmanis
[
Ros22
]
based on representation theory has been used to re-prove lower bounds for permutation inversion. Furthermore, a number of results from the QROM can be lifted to this setting
[
Cza+17
,
Cza+19
]
, as a random one-way permutation is quantum indistinguishable from a random function
[
Zha15
,
Yue13
]
.

1.4
Reader’s guide

This manuscript is written in order of mathematical dependence.
Sections

2
and
3
lay out the core ideas and notation, and should be read first. It may be helpful to read
Section

7
, especially
Section

7.1
, afterwards to build intuition about our construction, as the search applications are relatively simple. The application to Feistel in
Section

6
should be read after the soundness proof.

For the soundness proof,
Section

5.5
and the proof of the main theorem provides a high level overview of the pieces, and uses only notation from
Sections

4.1
and
5.1
.
Section

4
defines the purification of permutation oracles whose analysis forms the core of this work, though it is quite technical, and many of the definitions and lemmas may seem unmotivated if read immediately after
Section

3
.
Section

5
relates this purification to our compressed permutation oracle.

2
Preliminaries

Throughout this document, we will assume
N=4^{n}
is a power of four, and identify
[N]
with bit strings in the natural way. The choice of
4^{n}
reflects that we are constructing a
2n
bit permutation from
n
bit to
n
bit functions.

2.1
Quantum

Given a pure state
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}
, we denote the corresponding density matrix by
\rho(\ket{\psi})=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
. We recall the trace norm of a density matrix,
\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{1}=\mathrm{Tr}[\sqrt{\rho\rho^{\dagger}}]
. The corresponding trace distance of two mixed states upper bounds the probability that they can be distinguished by any algorithm.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\sigma,\tau
be density matrices over
\mathcal{H}
. Then, given either
\sigma
or
\tau
with equal probability, the maximum distinguishing advantage
D(\sigma,\tau)
is given by

\displaystyle D(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\sigma-\tau}\right\|_{1}.

(4)

For a tensor product Hilbert space, we use capital sans serif subscripts to label each factor, e.g.
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}}=\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}
. We also refer to the state as having a register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
and a register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}
. When an operator
O_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
acts on
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
, we can naturally extend it’s action to
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}}
as
O_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}
: we will drop this tensor product for convenience, writing
O_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}}
in place of
O_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}}
.

We will show that two oracles are indistinguishable by showing that the possible views of an adversary making queries to one of the two oracles are described by density matrices which are close in trace distance. Uhlmann’s theorem allows us to bound the trace distance of two density matrices by constructing an isometry on their purifications.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}
and
\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{C}}}
be normalized quantum states, and let
V:\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{C}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}
be an isometry. Let
\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}=\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}[\rho(\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}})]
and
\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}=\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{C}}}[\rho(\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AC}}})]
. Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}-\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}}-V_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{C}}}\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AC}}}}\right\|.

(5)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|{\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}-\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}[\rho(\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}})]-\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{C}}}[\rho(\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AC}}})]}\right\|_{1}

(6)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}[\rho(\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}})]-\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}[\rho(V_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{C}}}\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AC}}})]}\right\|_{1}

(
V
an isometry)

(7)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\rho(\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}})-\rho(V_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{C}}}\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AC}}})}\right\|_{1}

(
L_{1}
trace monotonicity)

(8)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}}-V_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{C}}}\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AC}}}}\right\|

(
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
relation)

(9)

∎

Another simple and useful fact is that the norm of an operator given by a direct sum is the maximum of the norm of any of the terms. If an operator
O:\mathcal{\mathcal{}}H(A)\otimes\mathcal{H}(B)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(A)\otimes\mathcal{H}(B)
can be written
O_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AB}}}=\bigoplus_{a\in A}|a\rangle\langle a|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes O_{a,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{B}}}
for some family of operators
O_{a}:\mathcal{H}(B)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(B)
, we will sometimes say that it is
controlled
on register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{H}_{k}
be a Hillbert space, and
O:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be an operator of the form
O=O_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus O_{k}
where
O_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{i}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{O}\right\|

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in[k]}\left\|{O_{i}}\right\|.

(10)

A projector
\Pi:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is an idempotent operator, where
\Pi^{2}=\Pi
. Projectors are orthogonal if they are Hermitian. We will only work with orthogonal projectors. We sometimes say
\ket{\psi}\in\Pi
to mean that
\Pi\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi}
.

2.2
Notation and definitions

Databases.

We use
\mathbf{F}_{M,N}
to denote the set of functions from
[M]
to
[N]
. We use
\mathbf{S}_{N}
to denote the set of permutations of
[N]
.
We use
\mathbf{D}_{M,N}
to denote the set of partial functions from
[M]
to
[N]
, or equivalently the set of functions from
[M]
to
[N]\cup\{\bot\}
. We use
\mathbf{I}_{M,N}
to denote set of partial
injective
functions from
[M]
to
[N]
, or equivalently the set of functions from
[M]
to
[N]\cup\{\bot\}
which are injective on all elements of the range except
\bot
.
6
6

6

As in, there may be multiple pre-images of
\bot
, but at most one preimage of any
y\in[N]
.

We will sometimes drop the subscript when it is clear from context. For our purposes, we will almost always work with
\mathbf{I}_{N,N}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\sqrt{N},\sqrt{N}}
, where we suppose
N=4^{n}
is a power of four. We take total functions to be a special case of partial functions, meaning

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{M,N}

\displaystyle\subset\mathbf{D}_{M,N}

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{N}

\displaystyle\subset\mathbf{I}_{N}.

(11)

We will interchange between the partial and total function representations, and refer to such objects as
databases
, which may or may not be injective. For a
D\in\mathbf{D}_{M,N}
, we use
\mathsf{Dom}(D)
to denote the domain of
D
as a partial function, or equivalently the preimages of
[N]
as a total function. The image
\mathsf{Im}(D)
refers to the image of
D
as a partial function, and similarly define
\mathsf{Dom}(I)
and
\mathsf{Im}(I)
for
I\in\mathbf{I}_{M,N}
. The size of a database can be defined as
|D|=|\mathsf{Dom}(D)|
, and similarly for injective databases
|I|=|\mathsf{Dom}(I)|
. When
M,N
are understood from context, we write
\mathbf{D}_{t}
to denote the set of
D\in\mathbf{D}
of size
|D|=t
, and similarly
\mathbf{D}_{\leq t}
to denote the set of
D\in\mathbf{D}
of size
|D|\leq t
. We similarly define
\mathbf{I}_{t}
and
\mathbf{I}_{\leq t}
.

We use
\boldsymbol{\bot}
to denote the trivial partial function, or equivalently the total function which maps all inputs to
\bot
.
Given a
D\in\mathbf{D}_{M,N}
, we use
D[x\rightarrow y]
to denote the the function

\displaystyle D[x\rightarrow y](x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}D(x^{\prime})&\text{(If $x\neq x^{\prime}$)}\\
y&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(12)

We similarly define
I[x\rightarrow y]
, with the subtlety that we may need to discard a conflicting element from the domain of
I
to ensure injectivity,

\displaystyle I[x\rightarrow y](x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}I(x^{\prime})&\text{(If $x\neq x^{\prime}$, and either $y=\bot$ or $I(x^{\prime})\neq y$)}\\
\bot&\text{(If $x\neq x^{\prime}$, $y\neq\bot$, and $I(x^{\prime})=y$)}\\
y&\text{(If $x=x^{\prime}$)}\end{cases}

(13)

We will almost always use this notation when
x
is not already in the domain, and in the case of injective functions where
y
is not already in the range. We can identify databases with relations as well, and write
(x,y)\in D
when
D(x)=y
, and similarly for
(x,y)\in I
.

Given a
D\in\mathbf{D}
, we define
D|^{x}
as the set of databases which agree with
D
on all inputs except
x
, in particular

\displaystyle D|^{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{D[x\rightarrow y]\,:\,y\in[N]\cup\{\bot\}\}.

(14)

We let
\mathbf{D}|^{x}
be the set of databases which are undefined on
x
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}|^{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{D\in\mathbf{D}\,:\,x\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)\}\,.

(15)

Note that
\{D|^{x}\,:\,D\in\mathbf{D}|^{x}\}
is a partition of
\mathbf{D}
for any fixed
x
. We define similar notions for injective databases
I\in\mathbf{I}
, once again taking care to maintain injectivity in the natural way.

\displaystyle I|^{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{I[x\rightarrow y]\,:\,y\in([N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I))\cup\{I(x)\}\cup\{\bot\}\}

(16)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}|^{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{I\in\mathbf{I}\,:\,x\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)\},

(17)

where once again
\{I|^{x}\,:\,I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}\}
is a partition of
\mathbf{I}
for any fixed
x
.
Given a set
S
, we denote a uniform random element of
S
by
x\sim S
. Given a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
, we use
x_{L}
to refer to the first
n
bits and
x_{R}
to refer to the last
n
bits.

Spaces.

Given a set
S
, we denote by
\mathcal{H}(S)
the Hilbert space spanned by an orthogonal basis labeled by elements of
S
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(S)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{span}\{\ket{x}\,:\,x\in S\},

\displaystyle\langle x|x^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{x,x^{\prime}}.

(18)

In such a space, for a set
A\subset S
we denote the uniform superposition of elements in
A
by

\displaystyle\ket{+_{A}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|A|}}\sum_{x\in A}\ket{x}.

(19)

Given sets
A_{1},\dots,A_{l}\subset S
such that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}A_{i}=S
and
A_{i}\cap A_{j}=\emptyset
for all
i\neq j
, we say that
\{A_{1},\dots,A_{l}\}
is a partition of
S
. In that case, we may express
\mathcal{H}(S)
as the direct sum
\mathcal{H}(A_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{H}(A_{l})
.

We will often deal with the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{D})
and
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})\leq\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{D})
. We define the projector
\Pi^{t}
and
\Pi^{\leq t}
on the former space as

\displaystyle\Pi^{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D}_{t}}|D\rangle\langle D|,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\leq t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{t}\Pi_{k}.

(20)

We can define these operators on
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
by their restriction to this subspace.

A norm without a subscript,
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|
, refers to the standard
L_{2}
norm. We use
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{1}
for the trace norm. For the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{D})
or
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
, for any Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
, we use the notation
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{\leq t}
for the standard
L_{2}
norm restricted to databases of size at most
t
. In other words, for a state
\ket{\psi}
or operator
O
on
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}=\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
, we define

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\|{\Pi^{\leq t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|,

\displaystyle\left\|{O_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\|{O_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\cdot\Pi^{\leq t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}}\right\|.

(21)

We also define consistency projectors for databases, which assert that a given input-output pair appears in the database. For databases over
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{F}_{M,N})
and an input-output pair
(x,y)\in[M]\times[N]
, we define

\displaystyle\Pi^{(x,y)\in\mathsf{db}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D}\,:\,D(x)=y}|D\rangle\langle D|,

(22)

and similarly for the space of injective databases.

2.3
Compressed oracles

In this section, we briefly recall the standard compressed oracle due to
[
Zha19
]
. Our presentation diverges from the original to more closely match the presentation of compressed permutation oracles. Throughout, we assume
M
and
N
are powers of two, and identify elements of
[M]
and
[N]
with bitstrings by the natural representation.

Let
f\sim\mathbf{F}_{M,N}
be a uniform random function, and consider the oracle

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{f}\ket{x,y}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{x,y\oplus f(x)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}.

(23)

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{f}}
be a quantum algorithm which makes
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}_{f}
. This algorithm is described by a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
for its internal state and query registers, as well as a sequence
A_{0},\dots,A_{q}
of unitary operators on
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
describing the action in between each query. We refer to registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}
as the query registers, corresponding to the two registers in
Equation

23
. For simplicity, we consider these registers to be inside of register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
. We can write the final mixed state of the algorithm as

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathcal{O}_{f})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{f\sim\mathbf{F}_{M,N}}\left[\rho(A_{q}\mathcal{O}_{f}\dots A_{1}\mathcal{O}_{f}A_{0}\ket{0})\right].

(24)

Alternatively, we could purify this experiment, defining a register
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{F})
to hold the purified function, and replacing the standard oracle with the purified oracle

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYF}}}\ket{x,y}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{x,y\oplus f(x)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}.

(25)

Considering the purified experiment over
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AF}}}
and tracing out
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}
results in the same view,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathcal{O}_{f})}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}\left(\rho\left(A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AF}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AF}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{+_{\mathbf{F}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}\right)\right).

(26)

The compressed picture.

The compressed oracle provides an alternative, powerful picture of quantum accessible random functions. In this picture, the purifying register is a database which begins empty. Each query modifies only the query point in the database, somewhat analogous to classical lazy sampling.

To define this formally, we will first consider the subspace
\mathcal{H}(D|^{x})\leq\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{D})
for some
D\in\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{D}_{M,N}
and
x\in[M]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(D)
. This subspace captures modifying the database on only the
x
-th position.
Let us write
\ket{+_{x,D}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{y\in[N]}\ket{D[x\rightarrow y]}
. We can now define the
function compression
operator
\mathsf{fC}
on this space, indexed by
x
and
D
, as

\displaystyle\mathsf{fC}_{x,D}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{1}-|+_{x,D}\rangle\langle+_{x,D}|-|D\rangle\langle D|+|+_{x,D}\rangle\langle D|+|D\rangle\langle+_{x,D}|.

(27)

In other words, this operator swaps the uniform superposition of outputs with the undefined output, for the input
x
. Recalling that
\{D|^{x}\,:\,D\in\mathbf{D}|^{x}\}
is a partition of
\mathbf{D}
, we may then define the full compression operator and its controlled analog as

\displaystyle\mathsf{fC}_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigoplus_{D\in\mathbf{D}|^{x}}\mathsf{fC}_{x,D},

\displaystyle\mathsf{fC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XD}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}=\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathsf{fC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}.

(28)

The final piece we will need is the extended purified oracle
\mathsf{P}
, which acts as

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}\ket{x,y}\ket{D}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\ket{x,y\oplus D(x)}\ket{D}&\text{(If $x\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)$)}\\
\ket{x,y}\ket{D}&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(29)

Now, we define the full compressed function oracle as the purified oracle conjugated by the compression operator, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathsf{cfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{fC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\mathsf{fC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger},

(30)

noting that
\mathsf{fC}
is an involution so the dagger is strictly unnecessary.
The compressed oracle experiment is somewhat similar to the purified experiment, except we allow the purifying register to include partial functions,
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{D})
. We refer to this register as the
compressed oracle
, and it is initialized to
\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}
. We additionally replace purified oracle calls with the compressed oracle
\mathsf{cfO}
. This corresponds to the final state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cfO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}\coloneqq A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}.

(31)

The following facts were shown by
[
Zha19
]
, and are core to the theory. At a high level, compressed oracles are:

1.

Sound
, in that they faithfully represent algorithms querying random functions.

2.

Bounded
, in that the size of the compressed database grows by at most one with each query.

3.

Meaningful
, in that the points defined by the database correspond to points the algorithm “knows”.

These key properties are formalized in the following lemmas.

Lemma 2.4

(Soundness)
.

The view of an algorithm querying the compressed oracle is the same as an algorithm querying a truly random function,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}^{(\mathcal{O}_{f})}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}\left[|\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cfO})}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cfO})}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}\right].

(32)

Lemma 2.5

(Bounded growth)
.

The final state of the compressed oracle after
q
queries is supported entirely on functions with domain size
q
,

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cfO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{D}_{\leq q}).

(33)

To establish the fundamental lemma (that formalizes the third property above), we will interpret the adversary as outputting a tuple of
l
input-output pairs. We will compare the probabilities that these pairs appear in the database with the probability that they agree with the oracle.
7
7

7

Sometimes, this lemma is formulated in the context of search games with a target predicate. Our version implies this application as a special case, as shown in
Lemma

7.1
.
To do this, we define the projector

\displaystyle\Pi^{(\mathsf{cfO})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})\}\in([M]\times[N])^{\times l}}\left(\Pi^{(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathsf{db}}\dots\Pi^{(x_{l},y_{l})\in\mathsf{db}}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}\otimes

(34)

\displaystyle\quad(|x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{l},y_{l}\rangle\langle x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{l},y_{l}|\otimes\mathbb{1})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}},

(35)

which asserts that the output of the adversary corresponds to input-output pairs which agree with the database.
We will also require the analogous operator that decompresses before each check,

\displaystyle\Pi^{(\mathcal{O}_{f})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})\}\in([M]\times[N])^{\times l}}\left(\mathsf{fC}_{x_{1}}\Pi^{(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathsf{db}}\mathsf{fC}_{x_{1}}^{\dagger}\dots\mathsf{fC}_{x_{l}}\Pi^{(x_{l},y_{l})\in\mathsf{db}}\mathsf{fC}_{x_{l}}^{\dagger}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}\otimes

(36)

\displaystyle\quad(|x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{l},y_{l}\rangle\langle x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{l},y_{l}|\otimes\mathbb{1})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}.

(37)

Because the compressed oracle is indistinguishable from a random oracle, this projector is equivalent to checking if the adversary output is consistent with the oracle.

Lemma 2.6

(Fundamental lemma)
.

The probability that an adversary finds
l
input-output pairs of the oracle is exponentially close to the probability that the corresponding pairs appears in the compressed database,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{(\mathcal{O}_{f})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cfO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\Pi^{(\mathsf{cfO})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cfO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AD}}}}\right\|+\sqrt{\frac{l}{N}}.

(38)

Further properties.

Another important property of compressed oracles is that they are
valid
, in that decompressing on any given input will cause the truth table to have a well-defined output. A simple example of a valid database is
\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}
. If we consider applying decompression
\mathsf{fC}_{x}^{\dagger}
to this state, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{fC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}^{\dagger}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[N]}\ket{(x,y)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}},

(39)

where we use relation notation for the database. It is clear from the above expression that the output of
x
is well defined. It is known that this property is preserved under compressed oracle queries.

To make this formal, we define the
compressed
and
decompressed
subspaces. The projector onto the former will be denoted by
\Xi
, and the latter by
\Gamma
. We define these operators for a fixed input
x
as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D}\text{ s.t. }x\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)}|D\rangle\langle D|,

\displaystyle\Xi_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{fC}_{x}\Gamma_{x}\mathsf{fC}_{x}^{\dagger}.

(40)

Note that the compression operator interchanges compressed and decompressed subspaces. We define the general operators as the products of these over all values of
x
,

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle\coloneqq\prod_{x\in[M]}\Gamma_{x},

\displaystyle\Xi

\displaystyle\coloneqq\prod_{x\in[M]}\Xi_{x}.

(41)

Each
\Gamma_{x}
and
\mathsf{fC}_{x}
commutes with
\Gamma_{x^{\prime}}
and
\mathsf{fC}_{x^{\prime}}
for
x\neq x^{\prime}
, so this is a well defined projector.
The full decompressed subspace is simply the projector onto all total functions, from which it is clear that the standard purified oracle maintains this subspace. Analogously, the compressed oracle maintains the compressed subspace. This guarantees that, after applying the
\mathsf{fC}_{x}
operator, the compressed oracle will have a well-defined output for
x
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For any
\ket{\psi}\in\Xi
, we have
\mathsf{cfO}\ket{\psi}\in\Xi
.

The following further properties of compressed oracles are not, to our knowledge, shown in the literature. However, they have straightforward proofs, which we include.

Lemma 2.8
.

Fix some input
x\in[M]
. For each
D\in\mathbf{D}|^{x}
, choose a set
S_{x,D}\subseteq[N]
such that
|S_{x,D}|\geq N-t
. Let
\ket{S_{x,D}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|S_{x,D}|}}\sum_{y\in S_{x,D}}\ket{D[x\rightarrow y]}
.

Define the operator

\displaystyle G_{x,D}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{1}-|D\rangle\langle D|-|S_{x,D}\rangle\langle S_{x,D}|+|D\rangle\langle S_{x,D}|+|S_{x,D}\rangle\langle D|,

(42)

\displaystyle G_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigoplus_{D\in\mathbf{D}|^{x}}G_{x,D}.

(43)

Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathsf{fC}_{x}-G_{x}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}\right).

(44)

Proof.

First, let us fix a database
D
with
x\not\in D
and use the shorthand
\ket{+_{x,D}}
for the even superposition
\sum_{y\in[N]}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}D[x\rightarrow y]
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{+_{x,D}}-\ket{S_{x,D}}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\not\in S_{x,D}}\frac{1}{N}+\sum_{y\in S_{x,D}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{|S_{x,D}|}}\right)^{2}

(45)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{N}+N\cdot\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-t}}\right)^{2}

(46)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{t}{N}\right).

(47)

We may then write

\displaystyle\mathsf{fC}_{x,D}-G_{x,D}=~

\displaystyle(|+_{x,D}\rangle\langle+_{x,D}|-|S_{x,D}\rangle\langle S_{x,D}|)+

(48)

\displaystyle\ket{D}(\bra{+_{x,D}}-\bra{S_{x,D}})+(\ket{+_{x,D}}-\ket{S_{x,D}})\bra{D}.

(49)

By inspecting each term and applying the triangle inequality, one can verify that

\left\|{\mathsf{fC}_{x,D}-G_{x,D}}\right\|=O(\sqrt{t/N})\,.

(50)

The claim now follows from
Lemma

2.3
.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Fix an input
x\in[N]
for a compressed database of a random function, and let
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{R}}
denote an ancillary register. Let
\ket{\phi_{y}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{R}}}
be a family of states indexed by
y\in[N]
which are pairwise orthogonal, and consider a state of the form

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{DR}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D},D(x)\neq\bot}\alpha_{D}\ket{D}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}\ket{\phi_{D(x)}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{R}}}.

(51)

In other words, there is a record of the output of
x
outside the database register. Then, for any operator
G_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}
of the form described in
Lemma

2.8
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{(\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{DR}}}-G_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}})\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{DR}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{N-t}}\right).

(52)

Proof.

We can write

\displaystyle\left\|{(\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{DR}}}-G_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}})\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{DR}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D},D(x)\neq\bot}\frac{\alpha_{D}}{\sqrt{|S_{x,D[x\rightarrow\bot]}|}}\left(\ket{D[x\rightarrow\bot]}-\ket{S_{x,D[x\rightarrow\bot]}}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}}\ket{\phi_{D(x)}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{R}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D},D(x)\neq\bot}\frac{\left|\alpha_{D}\right|^{2}}{|S_{x,D[x\rightarrow\bot]}|}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{N-t}}\right),

(53)

where the second line follows from the fact that states in the sum corresponding
D\neq D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{D}
will be orthogonal. To see this, observe that we could define an isometry which combined every input-output pair except
D(x)
from register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{D}}
, and determined
D(x)
from register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{R}}
, to construct
D
in a new register.
∎

3
Compressed permutation oracles

In this section, we present our compressed permutation oracle framework. The construction itself is similar to the function case, though establishing soundness turns out to be much more difficult. Our construction is similar to that of Unruh
[
Unr23
]
, though we explicitly and unitarily maintain injectivity of the database. We begin with basic definitions of permutation oracles.

Let
\varphi\sim\mathbf{S}_{N}
be a uniform random permutation, and consider the oracle

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}\ket{b,x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\ket{y}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{b,x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\ket{y\oplus\varphi^{1-2b}(x)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}},

(54)

for a bit
b
and
x,y\in[N]
. In other words,
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
is a standard oracle for the permutation
\varphi
with direction controlled by the bit
b
, on query point
x
. We do not explicitly consider controlled queries (i.e., the possibility of not querying the oracle), but we note that all our proofs apply to this case as well.
8
8

8

The easiest way to see this is to replace controlled queries with a controlled swap of the output register (holding
y
) with the uniform superposition
\ket{+_{[N]}}
, before and after the query. A standard oracle acts as identity when the output register is in uniform superposition.
We will throughout consider algorithms which may query both
\varphi
and
\varphi^{-1}
, as
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
exposes both directions.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}}
be a quantum algorithm that makes
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
. This algorithm is described by a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
for its internal state, as well as a sequence
A_{0},\dots,A_{q}
of unitary operators on
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
, describing the action in between each query. We can write the final mixed state of the algorithm as

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\varphi\sim\mathbf{S}_{N}}\left[\rho(A_{q}\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}\dots A_{1}\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}A_{0}\ket{0})\right].

(55)

The compressed picture.

Similar to the compressed function oracle, we will take a purifying database which begins empty. A query on input
x
will affect only the database entries involving
x
. In contrast to the function case, however, this modification will be
controlled
on the rest of the database to avoid collisions. This results in an operator which cannot be expressed as a direct product over database entries, which reflects the correlations between permutation outputs. Further, compression operators on different input points will no longer commute.

To formalize the action, fix for now some
I\in\mathbf{I}
and
x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)
, and consider the subspace
\mathcal{H}(I|^{x})\leq\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
. This subspace reflects modifying only the
x
-th query point, though in this case we will perform this modification controlled on the rest of the database.
Let us define

\ket{+_{x,I}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|I|}}\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}\ket{I[x\rightarrow y]}\,.

(56)

We can now define the
permutation compression
operator
\mathsf{pC}
on this subspace, indexed by
x
and
I
, as

\displaystyle\mathsf{pC}_{x,I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{1}-|+_{x,I}\rangle\langle+_{x,I}|-|I\rangle\langle I|+|+_{x,I}\rangle\langle I|+|I\rangle\langle+_{x,I}|.

(57)

In other words, this operator swaps the uniform superposition of
non-colliding
outputs with the undefined output, for the input
x
. Recalling that
\{I|^{x}\,:\,I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}\}
is a partition of
\mathbf{I}
, we may then define the full compression operator and its controlled variant as

\displaystyle\mathsf{pC}_{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigoplus_{I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}}\mathsf{pC}_{x,I},

\displaystyle\mathsf{pC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}=\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathsf{pC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}.

(58)

We recall the extended purified oracle
\mathsf{P}
, which acts as

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}\ket{x,y}\ket{I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\ket{x,y\oplus I(x)}\ket{I}&\text{(If $x\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)$)}\\
\ket{x,y}\ket{I}&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(59)

The final ingredient we need is the flip operator
\mathsf{F}
, which inverts the database (viewed as a partial function). In other words,
I^{-1}(y)=x\neq\bot
if and only if
I(x)=y
, else
I^{-1}(y)=\bot
. Here, the database is required to be injective, and so we can define the flip unitary

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}\ket{I}

\displaystyle=\ket{I^{-1}}.

(60)

The full compressed permutation oracle is similar to the compressed function oracle, except we maintain injectivity of the database. This allows us to invert before answering inverse queries.
In particular, we define the full compressed permutation oracle as

\displaystyle\mathsf{cpO}\ket{b}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{b}\otimes\begin{cases}\mathsf{pC}\cdot\mathsf{P}\cdot\mathsf{pC}^{\dagger}&\text{(If $b=0$)}\\
\mathsf{F}\cdot\mathsf{pC}\cdot\mathsf{P}\cdot\mathsf{pC}^{\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{F}^{\dagger}&\text{(If $b=1$)}\end{cases}

(61)

where again the operators
\mathsf{pC}
and
\mathsf{F}
are involutions, and so the dagger may be suppressed.
We take the purifying register to be
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
. We refer to this register as the
compressed permutation oracle
, and it is initialized to
\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}
. We additionally replace oracle calls with the compressed oracle
\mathsf{cpO}
. This corresponds to the final state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}.

(62)

Throughout the rest of this document, we will establish that this simple construction satisfies properties analogous to those of the compressed random oracle
[
Zha19
]
.

3.1
Properties

The key properties we will show are that our compressed oracle is:

1.

Sound
, in that it faithfully represent algorithms querying two-way accessible random permutations.

2.

Bounded
, in that the size of the compressed database grows at most linearly with the number of queries.

3.

Meaningful
, in that the points defined by the database correspond to points the algorithm “knows”.

It is fairly straightforward to establish bounded growth and the fundamental lemma, points (2) and (3) above, which we do in this section. For now, we only state soundness; proving it will be quite technically involved, and is the focus of the next two sections. Note also that soundness is only approximate.

Lemma 3.1

(Soundness)
.

The view of an efficient algorithm querying the compressed permutation oracle is negligibly close to the view of the same algorithm querying a truly random permutation, i.e.,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{A},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\operatorname{negl}}}}{{\,\approx\,}}\,\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}[\rho(\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}})].

(63)

Proof.

Follows from
Theorem

5.19
.
∎

Lemma 3.2

(Bounded growth)
.

The final state of the compressed permutation oracle after
q
queries is supported solely on databases of size
q
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\leq q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}=\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}.

(64)

Proof.

Suppose that the query register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
holds the state
\ket{0,x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}
. Then, the
\mathsf{cpO}
operator preserves the subspace
\mathcal{H}(I|^{x})
on the purifying
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}
register. It follows that the operator can increase the size of the purifying register by at most
1
. A similar argument follows for the case of an inverse query.
∎

To establish the fundamental lemma, we will once again interpret the adversary as outputting a tuple of
l
input-output pairs. We will compare the probabilities that these pairs appear in the database with the probability that they agree with the (compressed) oracle. To do this, we define the projector

\displaystyle\Pi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\sum_{\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})\}\in[N]^{\times 2l}}\Pi^{(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathsf{db}}\dots\Pi^{(x_{l},y_{l})\in\mathsf{db}}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\otimes

(65)

\displaystyle\quad(|x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{l},y_{l}\rangle\langle x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{l},y_{l}|\otimes\mathbb{1})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}.

(66)

We will also require the the analogous operator after decompressing,

\displaystyle\Pi^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})\}\in[N]^{\times 2l}}\left(\mathsf{pC}_{x_{1}}\Pi^{(x_{1},y_{1})\in\mathsf{db}}\mathsf{pC}_{x_{1}}^{\dagger}\dots\mathsf{pC}_{x_{l}}\Pi^{(x_{l},y_{l})\in\mathsf{db}}\mathsf{pC}_{x_{l}}^{\dagger}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\otimes

(67)

\displaystyle\quad(|x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{l},y_{l}\rangle\langle x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{l},y_{l}|\otimes\mathbb{1})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}.

(68)

Once soundness of the compressed permutation oracle is established, this check corresponds to verifying that the adversary output is consistent with the oracle.
We can now state and prove the fundamental lemma. Due to the non-commutativity of compression operators, the dependence on
l
is quadratically worse than in the function case, though this may not be tight.

Lemma 3.3

(Fundamental lemma)
.

The probability that an adversary finds some list of
l
input-output pairs of the oracle after
t
queries is exponentially close to the probability that the pair appears in the compressed database,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\Pi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|+\frac{l}{\sqrt{N-t-l}}.

(69)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|-\left\|{\Pi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{(\Pi^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}-\Pi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}})\ket{\psi_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|

(70)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\Pi^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}-\Pi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}.

(71)

Note that both these operators are controlled on
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
, so by
Lemma

2.3
it suffices to analyze the difference for some fixed set
\mathbf{x}=\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})\}
. We then obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}_{\mathbf{x},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}-\Pi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{\mathbf{x},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{l}\left\|{\Pi_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathsf{db},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}-\mathsf{pC}_{x_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\Pi_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathsf{db},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\mathsf{pC}_{x_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}}\right\|_{\leq t+l}

(72)

by repeated applications of triangle inequality. We can analyze each term individually. Note that the operator
\Pi_{(x,y)\in\mathsf{db},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}-\mathsf{pC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\Pi_{(x,y)\in\mathsf{db},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\mathsf{pC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}
preserves the subspace
\mathcal{H}(I|^{x})
for any
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
. Therefore, it suffices to consider the action on a state

\displaystyle\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)\cup\{\bot\}}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}\ket{I[x\rightarrow y^{\prime}]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},

(73)

for some
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
of size at most
t+l
. We write
s=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}
. We can then compute

\displaystyle\Pi_{(x,y)\in\mathsf{db},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{y}\ket{I[x\rightarrow y]}

(74)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\Pi_{(x,y)\in\mathsf{db},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\mathsf{pC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{pC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\left(\alpha_{y}+\frac{\alpha_{\bot}-s}{\sqrt{N-|\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\right)\ket{I[x\rightarrow y]}

(75)

\displaystyle=\left(\alpha_{y}+\frac{\alpha_{\bot}-s}{\sqrt{N-|\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\right)\cdot

\displaystyle\quad\left(\ket{I[x\rightarrow y]}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\ket{+_{x,I}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\ket{I}\right)

(76)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{y}\ket{I[x\rightarrow y]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}+O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\right).

(77)

where the last line follows from
\left|s\right|,\left|\alpha_{\bot}\right|\leq 1
. Recalling that
N-|\mathsf{Im}(I)|\geq N-l-t
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi_{(x,y)\in\mathsf{db},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}-\mathsf{pC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\Pi_{(x,y)\in\mathsf{db},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\mathsf{pC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-l-t}}\right),

(78)

which combined with
Equation

72
proves the claim.
∎

4
Purified permutations

In this section, we introduce the ensemble of permutations which we will analyze to prove both soundness of our construction and of Feistel, and prove properties about the purified ensemble. We use
\varphi
to denote the permutation which is being queried. We will write
\varphi
as a product of constituent permutations. Some of the constituent permutations will be highly structured, which will enable us to apply the standard compressed oracle theory.

In particular, we consider an ensemble defined by two random permutations
\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{S}_{N}
, which we refer to as the outer or twirling permutations, and three random functions
h,k,f:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, which we refer to as the internal or inner Feistel. We will always take
h,k,f
to be uniform random functions, but we do not yet commit to a particular distribution for
\pi
and
\omega
. This tuple defines the permutation
\varphi_{(\pi,\omega,h,k,f)}
. The formula for this permutation is
\varphi_{(\pi,\omega,h,k,f)}(x)=y
, where

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\pi(x),

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\omega^{-1}(y),

(79)

\displaystyle m\oplus u_{R}

\displaystyle=h(u_{L}),

\displaystyle v_{L}\oplus u_{L}

\displaystyle=k(m),

\displaystyle m\oplus v_{R}

\displaystyle=f(v_{L}).

(80)

This is depicted in
Figure

3
, along with the variables above. For a generic value on the left wire, we will usually use
w
or
l
; on the right wire, usually
z
or
r
.

Figure 3:
The masked feistel construction, with commonly chosen variable names in blue.

We call this construction
masked Feistel
, as the outer permutations will hide the structure of the internal Feistel construction. We will analyze this ensemble by purifying.
The purification register lives in the space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{S}_{N})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{F}_{\sqrt{N},\sqrt{N}})^{\otimes 3}
for the permutations
\pi,\omega
and functions
h,k,f
respectively. We label registers holding the parts of a purification by
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi,\Omega,H,K,F}}
respectively. For concreteness, it may help to imagine
\pi,\omega
being chosen at uniform random; this is the case we will analyze in our soundness proof. In this case, the permutation
\varphi
is uniform random, as it is the product of a uniform random permutation (say
\pi
) and an independently chosen permutation.

4.1
Query analysis

Let us define the purified query operator
\mathsf{mfP}
on this space to act as

\displaystyle\mathsf{mfP}\ket{x,y}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}\ket{\pi,\omega,h,k,f}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{x,y\oplus\varphi_{(\pi,\omega,h,k,f)}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}\ket{\pi,\omega,h,k,f}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(81)

Looking ahead, we will answer inverse queries by conjugating this operator by a flip of the truth table; for now we focus on forwards queries for simplicity. Let
\mathcal{D}
be the distribution on
\mathbf{S}_{N}^{\times 2}
from which the twirling permutations are drawn. We can write the initial purified state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{0}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2^{n})^{3\cdot 2^{n}}}}\sum_{h,k,f\in\mathbf{F}_{2^{n},2^{n}}}\ket{h,k,f}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(82)

Then, answering queries using the
\mathsf{mfP}
operator and tracing out the purifying register at the end of the experiment produces the same view as randomly selecting
\varphi
from the appropriate distribution.

Now, we may use compressed oracle representations for the internal functions
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}
. Answering a query on input
x
in this compressed picture can be thought of as the following out-of-place circuit:

1.

Compute
u=\pi(x)
into a fresh register

2.

Call the compressed oracle on
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}
with input
u_{L}
, and write the output
z
to a fresh register.

3.

Call the compressed oracle on
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}
with input
u_{R}\oplus z
, and write the output
w
to a fresh register.

4.

Call the compressed oracle on
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}
with input
u_{L}\oplus w
, write the output
z^{\prime}
to a fresh register.

5.

Compute
v=u\oplus(w\|(z\oplus z^{\prime}))
, and output
\omega(v)
.

6.

Uncompute all intermediate values.

However, this circuit has many redundant steps. For instance, at the end of step (2) the database in register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}
is compressed on input
u_{L}
. The next operation which acts on this register is a decompression on
u_{L}
, in step (6). These operations are inverses of one another, and so they cancel out. Similarly, step (2) writes
z
to a fresh wire, only for the next step to be a decompression conditioned on
z
. It will instead be easier to imagine performing the decompression in steps (2-4) without writing the result to a new register, nor performing the final compression in these steps. Then, the query can be answered in step (5) using information in the databases contain in registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}
. Finally, we undo by compressing steps (2-4), in the reverse order.

We will call this sequence of decompressions the
masked Feistel decompression
, writing
\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}
, and
\mathsf{mfC}
for the corresponding masked Feistel compression. At an intuitive level, this decompression operator performs the following, where we use
D_{h},D_{k},D_{f}
to denote the databases stored in
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H,K,F}}
respectively. We introduce some auxiliary variables only for notational convenience; one should not think of these as being stored in any register.

1.

Decompress
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}
on input
u_{L}
, where
u=\pi(x)
.

2.

Decompress
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}
on input
u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L})
.

3.

Decompress
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}
on input
u_{L}\oplus D_{k}(u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L}))
.

Note that the database entries referenced are guaranteed to exist here, as they were just decompressed, and the compressed oracle maintains validity. After this operator is performed, the value
\varphi(x)
is guaranteed to be determined, and can be XORed into the target query register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}
. Following this, undoing the above steps in reverse order will re-compress the database. This method of answering queries is equivalent to the out-of-place circuit described above.

We will now define the compression operator
\mathsf{mfC}
more formally. Let

\displaystyle U^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{fC}_{u_{L},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}

(83)

\displaystyle U^{(K)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}\ket{D_{h}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{D_{h}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\otimes\begin{cases}\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}&\text{(If $u_{L}\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{h})$)}\\
\mathsf{fC}_{u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L}),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(84)

\displaystyle U^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\ket{D_{h},D_{k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{D_{h},D_{k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}\otimes\begin{cases}\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}&\text{(If $u_{L}\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{h})$)}\\
\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}&\text{(If $u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L})\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})$)}\\
\mathsf{fC}_{u_{L}\oplus D_{k}(u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L})),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(85)

\displaystyle U_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq U^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}U^{(K)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}U^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}},

(86)

where
U_{u}
compresses the internal databases on input
u
. Note that the choice of
\mathbb{1}
in the above cases is arbitrary; as described above, when these operators are called in the context of the masked Feistel construction the values are guaranteed to exist, and so these cases do not occur.

We can now define
\mathsf{mfC}
by applying the twirl, to obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{mfC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi HKF}}}\ket{\pi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{\pi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes U_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}},

(87)

\displaystyle\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X\Pi HKF}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathsf{mfC}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi HKF}}}.

(88)

We also define an operator analogous to the flip operator, which we denote by
\mathsf{mfF}
, which acts as

\displaystyle\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\pi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\ket{\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Omega}}}\ket{D_{h}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{D_{k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}\ket{D_{f}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{\omega^{-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\ket{\pi^{-1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Omega}}}\ket{D_{f}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{D_{k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}\ket{D_{k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}},

(89)

in other words this operator inverts and exchanges both twirl permutations, and interchanges the databases in
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}
. This operator is an involution, and if a purification
P
represents permutation
\varphi_{P}
, then the flipped purification
P^{\prime}
where
\mathsf{mfF}\ket{P}=\ket{P^{\prime}}
represents the permutation
\varphi^{-1}_{P}=\varphi_{P^{\prime}}
. Now, we can define the
compressed masked Feistel oracle

\mathsf{cmfO}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{cmfO}\ket{b}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{b}\otimes\begin{cases}\mathsf{mfC}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}&\text{(If $b=0$)}\\
\mathsf{mfF}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{mfF}^{\dagger}&\text{(If $b=1$)}\end{cases}

(90)

Observe that in this case we have
\mathsf{mfC}\neq\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}
, as the order of compression/decompressions on the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}
databases are flipped. This is in contrast with the compressed function and compressed permutation oracles, where compression is an involution.

We use notation
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})\in\mathbf{D}_{2^{n},2^{n}}^{\times 3}
for the compressed databases of functions
h,k,f
, and think of
P=(\pi,\omega,D)
as a possible state of the purification registers. We use
\mathbf{P}
to denote set of possible purifications (i.e.
P\in\mathbf{P}
). The size of a purification is the size of its largest database,
|P|=\max\{|D_{h}|,|D_{k}|,|D_{f}|\}
. We will define a number of desirable properties of both the twirling permutations
(\pi,\omega)
and the internal Feistel databases
(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})
, beginning with the latter.

4.2
Internal Feistel

In this section, we will analyze the internal three rounds of Feistel. In particular, our main object of study will be triples
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})
of databases. A core concept is a chain, which is a set of database points which suffice to define an input-output pair of the Feistel permutation.

Definition 4.1
.

Fix a
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})
. We say there is a
chain
from
u
to
v
in
D
if
D_{h}(u_{L})\oplus u_{R}=D_{f}(v_{L})\oplus v_{R}=m
, and
u_{L}\oplus v_{L}=D_{k}(m)
. The values
(u,m,v)
define the chain, and
(u_{L},m,v_{L})
are the input points along the chain.

We also say that the
rightward semi-chain
from
u
is length
0
if
D_{h}(u_{L})=\bot
, is length
1
if
D_{k}(D_{h}(u_{L})\oplus u_{R})=\bot
, is of length
2
if
D_{f}(u_{L}\oplus D_{k}(D_{h}(u_{L})\oplus u_{R}))=\bot
, and is of length
3
(and therefore a full chain to some
v
) otherwise.

We say that
D

supports
the pair
(u,v)
if there is a chain from
u
to
v
.
We use
\mathsf{Supp}(D)
to denote the injective database supported by the
D
tuple, and
\mathsf{Dom}(D)
and
\mathsf{Im}(D)
to denote the domain and range of this database (i.e. we write
\mathsf{Dom}(D)
in place of
\mathsf{Dom}(\mathsf{Supp}(D))
). We write
D(u)
in place of
\mathsf{Supp}(D)(u)
, and similar for
D^{-1}(v)
. Finally, for
u\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)
we write
D[u\rightarrow\bot]
to denote
D
with the chain beginning at
u
removed.

Two chains
collide
if any of the function inputs along the chain collide.

Definition 4.2
.

If
(u,m,v)\neq(u^{\prime},m^{\prime},v^{\prime})
are distinct chains in
D
, then they
collide
if
m=m^{\prime}
,
u_{L}=u_{L}^{\prime}
, or
v_{L}=v_{L}^{\prime}
.

A database triple
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})
is
canonical
if no two chains in
D
collide, and every input output pair in each database participates in exactly one chain. The set of all canonical database triples is denoted
\mathbf{C}\subset\mathbf{D}_{2^{n},2^{n}}^{\times 3}
, and
\mathbf{C}_{t}
denotes the set of canonical database where each
D_{h}
,
D_{k}
, and
D_{f}
are of size
t
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})\in\mathbf{C}_{t}
be a canonical triple of databases of size
t
each. Then there are
t
full chains
u_{1}\rightarrow v_{1},\dots,u_{t}\rightarrow v_{t}
with distinct
u_{i}
and distinct
v_{i}
. There are
t(t-1)
length
2
rightward semi-chains,
t\cdot(2^{n}-t)
of length
1
, and
(2^{n}-t)\cdot 2^{n}
of length
0
. There are the same number of leftward semi-chains of the appropriate length.

Proof.

The set of chains being size
t
is the definition of a canonical database. To compute the number of length
2
semi-chains, we choose any
w\in D_{h}
, and
z
such that
z\oplus D_{h}(w)\in D_{k}
, but which does not lead to an already queried value in
D_{f}
. We know that
w=(u_{i})_{L},z=(u_{i})_{R}
satisfy the first two conditions but not the third, but if a different
z^{\prime}\neq z
lead to a queried value in
D_{f}
then we would have a colliding chain. This would violate canonicity, so we may choose any
z^{\prime}\neq z
, of which there are
t-1
satisfying the remaining constraints.

For the number of semi-chains of length
1
, we choose
w\in D_{h}
and any
z
s.t.
D_{h}(w)\oplus z\not\in D_{k}
. There are
t
choices for
w
, and
2^{n}-t
choices for
z
given
w
. Similarly, for semi-chains of length
0
we may pick any
w\not\in D_{h}
of which there are
2^{n}-t
, and any
z
of which there are
2^{n}
. We can verify that

\displaystyle t+t(t-1)+t\cdot(2^{n}-t)+(2^{n}-t)\cdot 2^{n}

\displaystyle=t^{2}+(t+2^{n})\cdot(2^{n}-t)

(91)

\displaystyle=2^{2n}.

(92)

∎

Given some injective database
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
, let
\mathbf{D}(I)
denote the set of canonical databases which support
I
. Note that this set is empty if any one of the following are true.

Definition 4.4
.

An
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
is
non-allowable
if any of the following predicates are true.

1.

There is an
input left collision
in
I
if there are distinct
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)
such that
x_{L}=x_{L}^{\prime}
.

2.

There is an
output left collision
in
I
if there are distinct
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Im}(I)
such that
y_{L}=y_{L}^{\prime}
.

3.

There is an
internal left collision
in
I
if there is an
(x,y)\in I,x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Dom}(I),y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Im}(I)
with
(x,y)\neq(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
and
x_{L}\oplus y_{L}=x^{\prime}_{L}\oplus y^{\prime}_{L}
.

We say
I
