is allowable if none of these events occur, i.e.
\mathbf{D}(I)\neq\emptyset
, and denote by
\mathbf{A}_{t}\subset\mathbf{I}_{t}
the set of allowable databases.
9
9

9

In fact, condition (3) entails conditions (1) and (2), though we explicitly state all three for clarity.

Lemma 4.5
.

Definition

4.4
characterizes all non-allowable databases.

Proof.

Suppose that
I
does not satisfy
Definition

4.4
. If there is an input or output left collision, then there cannot be disjoint chains for the left colliding inputs or outputs. Suppose then that there are not input or output left collisions, yet there is an internal collision. Let
(u,v)\in I,u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Dom}(I),v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Im}(I)
be such a collision. Suppose that
D
were a canonical database supporting
I
, such that there is a chain from
u
to
v
. We write this chain as

\displaystyle\underbrace{(u_{L},z_{h})}_{\in D_{h}}\mapsto\underbrace{(u_{R}\oplus z_{h},w_{k})}_{\in D_{k}}\mapsto\underbrace{(u_{L}\oplus w_{k},z_{f})}_{\in D_{f}},

(93)

where we observe that
w_{k}=u_{L}\oplus v_{L}
. Now observe that
u_{L}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{h})
and
v_{L}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{f})
, so let us define

\displaystyle z_{h}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=D_{h}(u_{L}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle z_{f}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=D_{f}(v_{L}^{\prime}).

(94)

Consider the inputs
u^{\prime\prime}=u_{L}^{\prime}\|(z_{h}^{\prime}\oplus u_{R}\oplus z_{h})
and
v^{\prime\prime}=v_{L}^{\prime}\|(z_{f}^{\prime}\oplus u_{R}\oplus z_{h})
. There is then a chain from
u^{\prime\prime}
to
v^{\prime\prime}
of the form

\displaystyle\underbrace{(u_{L}^{\prime\prime},z_{h}^{\prime})}_{\in D_{h}}\mapsto\underbrace{(u_{R}\oplus z_{h},w_{k})}_{\in D_{k}}\mapsto\underbrace{(v_{L}^{\prime\prime},z_{f}^{\prime})}_{\in D_{f}},

(95)

where we use the fact that
u_{L}^{\prime}\oplus w_{k}=v_{L}^{\prime}=v_{L}^{\prime\prime}
. This chain collides with the chain found from
u
to
v
. We know either
u^{\prime\prime}\neq u
(if
u^{\prime}\neq u
, and assumption of no input left collisions) or
v^{\prime\prime}\neq v
(if
v^{\prime}\neq v
, and assumption of no output left collisions), so there is a colliding chain in
D
, a contradiction.

Now let us consider the other direction. Suppose that
I
does satisfy
Definition

4.4
, we will construct a database
D
which supports exactly
I
and is canonical. Fix some ordering on the input output pairs as
I=(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2}),\dots,(u_{t},v_{t})
. Then, beginning with an empty
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})
, iteratively construct the database as in
Algorithm

1
.

For every
j\in[t]
:

1

Choose some
z_{j}
such that
(u_{j})_{R}\oplus z_{j}\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})
, and add
((u_{j})_{L},z_{j})
to
D_{h}
.

2

Add
((u_{j})_{R}\oplus z_{j},(u_{j})_{L}\oplus(v_{j})_{L})
to
D_{k}
.

3

Add
((v_{j})_{L},(v_{j})_{R}\oplus z_{j}\oplus(u_{j})_{R})
to
D_{f}
.

Algorithm 1

Database extension

After
j-1
iterations, step (2) has
2^{n}-j+1
choices, and hence will succeed (unless
t\geq 2^{n}
, in which case database states are anyways not defined). If step (2) succeeds, then it will create new length
1
right facing semi-chains beginning at
(u_{j})_{L}\|z_{R}
for every
z_{R}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Of these,
j-1
will be extended immediately to length
2
, corresponding to the values already in
D_{k}
. On the left wire, these length
2
right facing semi-chains lead to the values
(u_{j})_{L}\oplus(u_{i})_{L}\oplus(v_{i})_{L}
for
i\in[j-1]
. From the allowability of
I
, we know that these values will never be extended to full length
3
chains, as
(u_{j})_{L}\oplus(u_{i})_{L}\oplus(v_{i})_{L}\neq(v_{p})_{L}
for any
p\in[t]
.

Step (3) will add a new pair to
D_{k}
if step (2) succeeds, and of the previously described length
1
right facing semi-chains, will extend only the one beginning at
u_{j}=(u_{j})_{L}\|(u_{j})_{R}
, as desired.
However, it will also extend length
1
right facing semi-chains of the form
(u_{i})_{L}\|(z_{i}\oplus(u_{j})_{R})
, for every
i\in[j-1]
. These now-length-
2
right facing semi-chains will lead to a value
(u_{i})_{L}\oplus(u_{j})_{L}\oplus(v_{j})_{L}
on the left wire after the application of
h
and
k
. However, we know that this value is distinct from all
(v_{p})_{L}
for any
p\in[t]
, by the fact that
I
has no internal left collisions. Hence, these chains are never extended to length
3
chains. Therefore, each iterations adds a chain from
u_{j}
to
v_{j}
, disjoint from all prior chains, and adds no other chains. The databases are clearly minimal at the end of this procedure, so
D
is a canonical database tuple supporting
I
.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

For any
I\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
, we have the equation
|\mathbf{D}(I)|=\binom{2^{n}}{t}
.

Proof.

Consider the algorithm described in the proof of
Lemma

4.5
. In the first iteration of step (1), there are
2^{n}
choices, in the second,
2^{n}-1
, and etc. up to
2^{n}-t+1
in the final. Any choice which is not of the form described leads immediately to a chain collision, and hence is not allowable. Steps (2) and (3) do not allow any choices, as the values in
D_{k}
and
D_{f}
are constrained by the value of
v
. However, the choice of ordering of input-output pairs is arbitrary, leading to a factor
t!
redundancy, and therefore a total of
\binom{2^{n}}{t}
databases consistent with
I
.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

Let
A\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
, and
u\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)
. We say that
A

allows

u
if there exists a
v\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
such that
A[u\rightarrow v]\in\mathbf{A}_{t+1}
. We denote this as
A\heartsuit u
, and if
A[u\rightarrow v]
remains allowable then we write
A\heartsuit(u,v)
and say
(u,v)
is allowed.

Remark 4.8
.

If
A
allows
u
, then
u^{\prime}
with
u^{\prime}_{L}=u_{L}
is allowed as well. There are at least
2^{n}-t^{2}-t
allowable left substrings, by a direct counting argument.

Definition 4.9
.

Fix some
A\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
and
u\in\{0,1\}^{n}
which
A
allows. Let
\mathbf{V}_{u,A}
denote the set of values
v\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
such that
A[u\rightarrow v]
is allowable, and let
\mathbf{L}_{u,A}
denote the set of left substrings in
\mathbf{V}_{u,A}
. Formally:

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{u,A}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{v\,:\,A[u\rightarrow v]\in\mathbf{A}_{t+1}\},

(96)

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{u,A}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{v_{L}\,:\,v\in V_{u,A}\}.

(97)

We will sometimes use a length
n
bitstring
l\in\{0,1\}^{n}
as the index
\mathbf{L}_{l,A}
or
\mathbf{V}_{l,A}
. This is well defined, as these sets only depend on the left
n
bits of
u
.

Lemma 4.10
.

For any
A\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
and
u\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(A)
which
A
allows, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{L}_{u,A}|\geq 2^{n}-2t^{2}-2t,

(98)

and further for any
l\in\mathbf{L}_{u,A}
and
r\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we have
l\|r\in\mathbf{V}_{x,A}
.

Proof.

The second statement is straightforward, as allowability does not constrain the right substring in any way. For the first, observe that an assignment
A[u\rightarrow v]
can cause an internal left collision in one of two ways:

1.

There exists
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Dom}(A)\cup\{u\}
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Im}(A)\cup\{v\}
with
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\neq(u,v)
such that

\displaystyle u_{L}^{\prime}\oplus v_{L}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=u_{L}\oplus v_{L}

(99)

2.

There exists
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Dom}(A)\cup\{u\}
,
(u^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\in A
such that

\displaystyle u_{L}^{\prime}\oplus v_{L}

\displaystyle=u_{L}^{\prime\prime}\oplus v_{L}^{\prime\prime}

(100)

Note that in case (1), when
v=v^{\prime}
the equation reduces to the equality
u_{L}^{\prime}=u_{L}
, which is not true by the fact that
A
allows
u
. Hence, there are
t^{2}+t
equations for the first (
t+1
for
u^{\prime}
,
t
for
v^{\prime}
), which therefore eliminate at most
t^{2}+t
possible values for
v_{L}
. Similarly, there are
t+1
choices for
u^{\prime}
and
t
choices for
(u^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime\prime})
in case (2), giving at most
2t^{2}+2t
eliminated choices of
v_{L}
. Recall that the lack of internal left collisions implies no output left collisions (in particular, case (1) with
u=u^{\prime}
gives
v_{L}\neq v_{L}^{\prime}
for all
v\in\mathsf{Im}(A)
), so any choice not excluded leads to an allowable database.
∎

Database extensions.

Now we turn to studying subsets of three round databases which can occur as intermediate states between canonical databases. We will refer to these as extensions. At a high level, these sets represent databases which would be canonical, except for one chain which is only partially defined.

Definition 4.11
.

We say that a tuple
D
is a
one-extension
if there exists:

1.

some canonical
D^{\prime}=(D_{h}^{\prime},D_{k}^{\prime},D_{f}^{\prime})
,

2.

some
l\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
D^{\prime}
allows
l\|r
for some
r
, yet
l\|r\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D^{\prime})
for any
r
,

3.

some
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

such that
D=(D_{h}^{\prime}[l\rightarrow z],D_{k}^{\prime},D_{f}^{\prime})
.

We would say that
D
above is a one-extension of
D^{\prime}
with
(l,z)
. We use
\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{C}_{t})
to denote the set of one-extensions of size
t
canonical databases, and
\mathbf{E}(D)
to denote the set of one-extensions of
D
. We define two-extensions similarly.

Definition 4.12
.

We say that a tuple
D
is a
two-extension
if there exists:

1.

some
D^{\prime}
which is a one-extension of
D^{\prime\prime}
with
(l,z)
,

2.

some
m\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
m\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k}^{\prime}
),

3.

some
w\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
l\oplus w\in\mathbf{L}_{l,A(D^{\prime\prime})}
,

such that
D=(D_{h}^{\prime},D_{k}^{\prime}[m\rightarrow w],D_{f}^{\prime})
.

We would say that
D
above is a two-extension of
D^{\prime\prime}
with
(l,z),(m,w)
, and a one-extension of
D^{\prime}
with
(m,w)
. We use
\mathbf{E}^{2}(\mathbf{C}_{t})
to denote the set of two-extensions of size
t
canonical databases,
\mathbf{E}^{2}(D)
to denote the set of two-extensions of a
D\in\mathbf{C}
, and
\mathbf{E}(D^{\prime})
to denote the set of two-extensions obtained as one-extensions of
D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{C})
. Finally, we define a three extension,

Definition 4.13
.

We say that a tuple
D
is a
three-extension
if there exists:

1.

some
D^{\prime}
which is a two-extension of
D^{\prime\prime}
with
(l,z),(m,w)
,

2.

some
z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

such that
D=(D_{h}^{\prime},D_{k}^{\prime},D_{f}^{\prime}[l\oplus w\rightarrow z^{\prime}])
.

Similarly, we use
\mathbf{E}^{3}(\mathbf{C}_{t})
to denote three-extensions of canonical databases,
\mathbf{E}^{3}(D)
for
D\in\mathbf{\mathbf{}}C_{t}
to denote three-extensions of
D
,
\mathbf{E}^{2}(D)
for
D\in\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{C}_{t})
to denote three-extensions obtained as two-extensions of a fixed one-extension, and
\mathbf{E}(D)
for
D\in\mathbf{E}^{2}(\mathbf{C}_{t})
to denote three-extensions obtained as one-extensions of a fixed two-extension.

Lemma 4.14
.

Fix some
u\in[N]
. For any
t\geq 0
, canonical
D\in\mathbf{C}_{t+1}
with
u\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)
, there exists a unique
D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}_{t}
with
u\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)
such that
D\in\mathbf{E}^{3}(D^{\prime})
. Additionally, all three-extensions of canonical databases are themselves canonical.

Proof.

For the first statement, consider the database
D^{\prime}
obtained by removing from
D
the chain beginning at
u
. Canonicity is preserved under removing a chain (this cannot add a chain collision, or make the database non-minimal), so
D\in\mathbf{E}^{3}(D^{\prime})
. Further, every
D^{\prime\prime}\neq D^{\prime}
will have either a chain beginning at
u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)
and ending at a
v^{\prime}\neq D(u^{\prime})
, or a chain beginning at some
u^{\prime\prime}\neq u
and
u^{\prime\prime}\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)
. In either case, no extension can remove this chain, and such a chain does not appear in
D
, so
D^{\prime\prime}\not\in\mathbf{C}^{3}(D^{\prime})
.

For the second statement, recall the correctness of
Algorithm

1
, proved in
Lemma

4.5
. In particular, an extension can be seen as one iteration of said algorithm, over all possible allowable
(u,v)
pairs.
∎

Now we prove some disjointness properties for extension sets. These properties will be useful later on to argue that the span of various extension sets will be orthogonal, and hence certain operators can be written in a direct sum manner. This in turn will be key to analyzing the complicated effect of compression operators on the databases when they are entangled with the other registers. Towards this, we introduce the pipe notation for database triples. We use an upwards arrow
\uparrow
for subsets that will occur while evaluating forwards and/or adding output points, and a downwards arrow
\downarrow
for subsets that occur while evaluating backwards and/or removing output points.

Definition 4.15
.

Fix some
D\in\mathbf{C}_{t}
and
u\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(D)
which
D
allows. Then we define

\displaystyle D\uparrow^{u}_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{D\}\cup\{(D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z],D_{k},D_{f})\,:\,z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{u_{R}\oplus\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\}\}.

(101)

For some
D^{\prime}\in D\uparrow^{u}_{h}\subset\mathbf{E}(D)
with extension
(u_{L},z)
and
z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{u_{R}\oplus\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\}
, define

\displaystyle D^{\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{D^{\prime}\}\cup\{(D_{h}^{\prime},D_{k}^{\prime}[u_{R}\oplus z\rightarrow w],D_{f}^{\prime})\,:\,w\in\{u_{L}\oplus\mathbf{L}_{u,\mathsf{Supp}(D)}\}\}.

(102)

For some
D^{\prime\prime}\in D^{\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{k}\subset\mathbf{E}^{2}(D)
with extension
(u_{L},z),(u_{R}\oplus z,w)
as above, we define

\displaystyle D^{\prime\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{f}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{D^{\prime\prime}\}\cup\{(D_{h}^{\prime\prime},D_{k}^{\prime\prime},D_{f}^{\prime\prime}[u_{L}\oplus w\rightarrow z^{\prime}])\,:\,z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\}.

(103)

When this notation is applied to sets of databases, it is the union of the application to each individual one. We will collapse the notation
(D\uparrow^{u}_{h})\uparrow^{u}_{k}=D\uparrow^{u}_{h,k}
, and similarly
((D\uparrow^{u}_{h})\uparrow^{u}_{k})\uparrow^{u}_{f}=D\uparrow^{u}_{h,k,f}
.

Lemma 4.16
.

For any
D\neq D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}
and
u\in[N]
which is allowed by
D
and
D^{\prime}
but supported by neither, meaning
u\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)\cup\mathsf{Dom}(D^{\prime})
, we have

\displaystyle D\uparrow^{u}_{h}\cap D^{\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{h}

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

\displaystyle D\uparrow^{u}_{h,k}\cap D^{\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{h,k}

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

\displaystyle D\uparrow^{u}_{h,k,f}\cap D^{\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{h,k,f}

\displaystyle=\emptyset.

(104)

Proof.

There is WLOG a chain in
D
but not
D^{\prime}
that does not involve
u
, and this chain cannot be removed by any extension, nor can it be added by any extension that does not originate from
u
.
∎

Now we turn to defining and analyzing the downwards arrow notation. In contrast to the upwards arrow, the downwards arrow notation should be thought of as changing or removing an existing input-output point.

Definition 4.17
.

Let
D\in\mathbf{C}
and
u\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)
. Then we define

\displaystyle D\downarrow^{u}_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z],D_{k},D_{f})\,:\,z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\cup\{\bot\}\setminus\{u_{R}\oplus\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\}\}

(105)

\displaystyle D\downarrow^{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(D_{h},D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L})\rightarrow w],D_{f})\,:\,w\in\{u_{L}\oplus\mathbf{L}_{u,D[u\rightarrow\bot]}\}\cup\{\bot\}\}

(106)

\displaystyle D\downarrow^{u}_{f}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f}[D(u)_{L}\rightarrow z])\,:\,z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\cup\{\bot\}\}

(107)

Lemma 4.18
.

For any
D\neq D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}
and
u\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)\cap\mathsf{Dom}(D^{\prime})
, we have the disjointness relations

\displaystyle D\downarrow^{u}_{h}\cap D^{\prime}\downarrow^{u}_{h}

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

\displaystyle D\downarrow^{u}_{k}\cap D^{\prime}\downarrow^{u}_{k}

\displaystyle=\emptyset.

(108)

The identity
D\downarrow^{u}_{f}\cap D^{\prime}\downarrow^{u}_{f}=\emptyset
holds if
D[u\rightarrow\bot]\neq D^{\prime}[u\rightarrow\bot]
.

Proof.

Let us begin with the first equation. Observe that such a pair
D\neq D^{\prime}
has either disjoint chains not involving
u
, or
D(u)\neq D^{\prime}(u)
. Let us begin with the first case. In this case, changing the image of
u_{L}
in
D_{h}
as described (or making it undefined) will modify the chain from
u
to
D(u)
and/or
u
to
D^{\prime}(u)
, changing the last
n
bits of the preimage. However, it will not create any further chains, as
D_{h}(u_{L})
is chosen such that it does not connect to any other pair in
D_{k}
. Hence, the disjoint chain not involving
u
will remain, and the databases will be distinct.

In the other case, consider reassignments of the form
D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z]
and
D_{h}^{\prime}[u_{L}\rightarrow z^{\prime}]
. In such a case, there may be no chain from
u
to either
D(u)
or
D^{\prime}(u)
in the redefined
D
or
D^{\prime}
respectively. If one of
z
or
z^{\prime}
is
\bot
, observe that there are exactly two input-output pairs,
(r,w)\in D_{k}
and
(u_{L}\oplus w,z_{f})\in D_{f}
which do not participate in a chain. We have
u_{L}\oplus w\|r\oplus z_{f}=D(u)
. Similarly, under the redefinition
D_{h}^{\prime}[u_{L}\rightarrow z]
there are exactly two input-output pairs,
(r^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in D_{k}^{\prime}
and
(u_{L}\oplus w^{\prime},z_{f}^{\prime})\in D_{f}^{\prime}
which do not participate in a chain. We have
u_{L}\oplus w^{\prime}\|r^{\prime}\oplus z_{f}^{\prime}=D^{\prime}(u)\neq D(u)
. Therefore, these databases must be distinct. Similarly, if neither
z
nor
z^{\prime}
is
\bot
, there will remain some chain to
D(u)
in the first set and to
D^{\prime}(u)\neq D(u)
in the second.

Now let us turn to the middle statement. Similar to before, the claim is easily handled if there is disjoint chains in
D,D^{\prime}
not involving
u
, or if the reassignment is to
\bot
in either case. In the other case, we perform the non-trivial reassignment
D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L})\rightarrow w^{\prime\prime}]
and
D_{k}^{\prime}[u_{R}\oplus D_{h}^{\prime}(u_{L})\rightarrow w^{\prime\prime\prime}]
, and have
D(u)\neq D^{\prime}(u)
. In this case, there are exactly two input-output pairs,
(u_{L},z)\in D_{h}
and
(u_{L}\oplus w,z_{f})\in D_{f}
which do not participate in a chain for the former. Observe that these pairs would uniquely be completed to a chain under undoing the reassignment, i.e.
D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus z\rightarrow w]=D_{k}
. Under this unique completion, we would naturally obtain endpoint
D(u)
for the chain beginning at
u
. Similarly, there is a unique completion of the modified
D^{\prime}
, whose endpoint would be
D^{\prime}(u)
. These unique completions lead to distinct chains, and so the databases cannot be equal.

The final case follows similarly to the previous two: by the assumption that
D[u\rightarrow\bot]\neq D^{\prime}[u\rightarrow\bot]
there are distinct chains not involving
u
in
D
and
D^{\prime}
, which will not be modified by the described reassignment in
D_{f}
. It is worth observing that if
D(u)_{L}=D^{\prime}(u)_{L}
, on the other hand, then the claim fails to hold.
∎

With these in place, orthogonality of the spaces spanned by these sets follows. In the following lemma, for some
S\subset\mathbf{D}
we take
\mathcal{H}(S)\leq\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{D})
as a subspace.

Corollary 4.19

(of
Lemmas

4.16
and
4.18
)
.

For any
D\neq D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}
and
u\in[N]
which is allowed by
D
and
D^{\prime}
but supported by neither, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(D\uparrow^{u}_{h})

\displaystyle\perp\mathcal{H}(D^{\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{h}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(D\uparrow^{u}_{h,k})

\displaystyle\perp\mathcal{H}(D^{\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{h,k}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(D\uparrow^{u}_{h,k,f})

\displaystyle\perp\mathcal{H}(D^{\prime}\uparrow^{u}_{h,k,f}).

(109)

Further, for any
D\neq D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{C}
and
u\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)\cap\mathsf{Dom}(D^{\prime})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(D\downarrow^{u}_{h})

\displaystyle\perp\mathcal{H}(D^{\prime}\downarrow^{u}_{h}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(D\downarrow^{u}_{k})

\displaystyle\perp\mathcal{H}(D^{\prime}\downarrow^{u}_{k}),

(110)

and
\mathcal{H}(D\downarrow^{u}_{f})\perp\mathcal{H}(D^{\prime}\downarrow^{u}_{f})
when
D[u\rightarrow\bot]\neq D^{\prime}[u\rightarrow\bot]
.

We can now introduce the notation of assignment for database triples. For
u\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D)
and
v\not\in\mathsf{Im}(D)
with
(u,v)
allowable, we define
D[u\rightarrow v]
as the subset of
\mathbf{E}^{3}(D)
which contains a chain from
u
to
v
. In other words,

\displaystyle D[u\rightarrow v]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{D^{\prime}\,:\,D^{\prime}(u)=v,D^{\prime}[u\rightarrow\bot]=D\}.

(111)

Corollary 4.20

(of
Lemma

4.14
)
.

If
D\neq D^{\prime}
and both allow
(u,v)
, then
D[u\rightarrow v]\cap D^{\prime}[u\rightarrow v]=\emptyset
.

These extension sets will allow us to define a modified version of masked Feistel compression, which is close in operator norm to the original but easier to analyze.

4.3
Canonical compression

In this section, we describe an alternative to the
U_{u}
compression operator, which we will label by
G_{u}
and refer to as
canonical compression
. This operator is constructed such that it maintains canonicity of the database, as long as the input
u
is allowed. In order to define it, we will require notation for the set of ouput values which we can insert without resulting in an internal collision. Let us fix some databases
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})
with
D(u)=\bot
, and write the sets

\displaystyle H(u,D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{z\,:u_{R}\oplus z\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\}

(112)

\displaystyle K(u,D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{w\,:u_{L}\oplus w\in\mathbf{L}_{u,\mathsf{Supp}(D)}\},

(113)

where
\mathbf{L}_{u,\mathsf{Supp}(D)}
is as defined in
Section

4.2
. When we use
\mathbf{L}_{u,\mathsf{Supp}(D^{\prime})}
for
D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{E}(D)
, this is equivalent to
\mathbf{L}_{u,\mathsf{Supp}(D)}
.
We can define compression operators which swap the uniform superposition over values in this set with the undefined label, rather than the full superposition. To formalize this, fix some
D_{h}\in\mathbf{D}|^{u_{L}}
and some
D_{k}\in\mathbf{D}
, to define

\displaystyle\ket{+_{u_{L},H(u,D_{k})}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|H(u,D_{k})|}}\sum_{z\in H(u,D_{k})}\ket{D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z]}

(114)

\displaystyle G^{(H)}_{u,D_{h},D_{k},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\big(\mathbb{1}-|+_{u_{L},H(u,D_{k})}\rangle\langle+_{u_{L},H(u,D_{k})}|-|D_{h}\rangle\langle D_{h}|+

(115)

\displaystyle\qquad|+_{u_{L},H(u,D_{k})}\rangle\langle D_{h}|+|D_{h}\rangle\langle+_{u_{L},H(u,D_{k})}|\big)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}

\displaystyle G^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}\ket{D_{k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigoplus_{D_{h}\in\mathbf{D}|^{u_{L}},D_{k}\in\mathbf{D}}|D_{k}\rangle\langle D_{k}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}\otimes G^{(H)}_{u,D_{h},D_{k},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}.

(116)

We similarly define a canonical compression operator for the
K
register, first by fixing some
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})\in\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{C})
with
D_{k}(u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L}))=\bot
; we use
m=u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L})
going forward, and defining

\displaystyle\ket{+_{z,K(u,D)}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{|K(u,D)|}}\sum_{w\in K(u,D)}\ket{D_{h}[z\rightarrow w]}

(117)

\displaystyle G^{(K)}_{u,D,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\big(\mathbb{1}-|+_{m,K(u,D)}\rangle\langle+_{m,K(u,D)}|-|D_{k}\rangle\langle D_{k}|+\\
|+_{m,K(u,D)}\rangle\langle D_{k}|+|D_{k}\rangle\langle+_{m,K(u,D)}|\big)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}&\text{(If $D_{h}(u_{L})\neq\bot$)}\\
\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(118)

\displaystyle G^{(K)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\ket{D_{k}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{1}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\in\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{C})\\
\text{ s.t. }D_{k}(u_{R}\oplus D_{h}(u_{L}))=\bot\end{subarray}}|D_{h},D_{f}\rangle\langle D_{h},D_{f}|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HF}}}\otimes G^{(K)}_{u,D_{h},D_{f},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}.

(119)

Note that this is a unitary operator, as
K(u,D)
does not depend on
D_{k}(m)
, which cannot participate in a chain as it is
\bot
.
We take
G^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}=U^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}
, and define the full canonical compression operator

\displaystyle G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=G^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}G^{(K)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}G^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(120)

Remark 4.21
.

The canonical compression operators are exponentially close to the standard compression operators after a bounded number of queries. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{G^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}-U^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{2^{n}}}\right),

\displaystyle\left\|{G^{(K)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-U^{(K)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right),

(121)

\displaystyle\left\|{G^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-U^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\left\|{G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-U_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(122)

Proof.

The two equations on the top line follow from
Lemma

2.8
, and the bottom left by definition. Then, the bottom right follows from the other three equations by a simple sequence of triangle inequalities.
∎

Properties.

We can work out the action of
G_{u}
and its components in some examples. These facts will be useful in proving soundness of our compressed permutation oracle.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
A\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
be an allowable, size
t
injective database and let
u\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(A)
be an input which
A
allows. Then we have

\displaystyle G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A)}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{V}_{u,A}|}}\sum_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(123)

Proof.

Let us fix some
D=(D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})
which is canonical with
\mathsf{Supp}(D)=A
, and compute the action on such a state. We have

\displaystyle G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\ket{D}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2^{n}-t)\cdot|\mathbf{L}_{u,\mathsf{Supp}(D)}|\cdot 2^{n}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\oplus u_{R}\},\\
w\in\{\mathbf{L}_{u,\mathsf{Supp}(D)}\oplus u_{L}\},\\
z_{2}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\end{subarray}}\ket{D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z_{1}]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\otimes

(124)

\displaystyle\quad\ket{D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus z_{1}\rightarrow w]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}\ket{D_{f}[u_{L}\oplus w\rightarrow z_{2}]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}.

(125)

This state can be seen to be the uniform superposition of canonical databases which support
A[u\rightarrow v]
for some
v
, and agree with
D
10
10

10

Here we say database triples
D,D^{\prime}
agree if for every
w\in\mathsf{Dom}(D_{h})\cap\mathsf{Dom}(D_{h}^{\prime})
, we have
D_{h}(w)=D_{h}^{\prime}(w)
, and similarly for
D_{f}
and
D_{k}
.
, i.e. the set
D\uparrow_{h,k,f}^{u}
. Now observe that every canonical
D^{\prime}
supporting
A[u\rightarrow v]
for some
v
will agree with one, and only one, canonical
D
supporting
I
, from
Lemma

4.14
. Hence, when we extend the above calculation to the uniform superposition of all canonical
D
supporting
I
, we find

\displaystyle G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A)}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{V}_{u,A}|}}\sum_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(126)

∎

Lemma 4.23
.

Fix some
A\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
and
u\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(A)
which
A
allows, to write the state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\alpha_{v}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(127)

Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}^{(H)}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}-t}}\right).

(128)

Proof.

Let us consider some
D\in\mathbf{D}(A)
, and take
|A|=t
. We can write the component of
\ket{\psi}
which is an extension of
D
as

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{E}^{3}(D))}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

(129)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot(2^{n}-t)}}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{u_{R}\oplus\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\}}\ket{D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\otimes

(130)

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\alpha_{v}\ket{D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus z\rightarrow u_{L}\oplus v_{L}],D_{f}[v_{L}\rightarrow u_{R}\oplus z\oplus v_{R}]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{KF}}}.

(131)

Further, recall from
Lemma

4.14
we can write

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D}(A)}\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}},

(132)

so by linearity it suffices to analyze each component separately. Let us define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{D}=\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\mathcal{H}(D[u\rightarrow v]\downarrow^{u}_{h}),

(133)

where we have
\mathcal{H}_{D}\perp\mathcal{H}_{D^{\prime}}
for
D\neq D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{D}(A)
from
Corollaries

4.20
and
4.18
. Observe that
\ket{\psi_{D}}\in\mathcal{H}_{D}
, and furthermore the image under
G^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}
will remain in this space. Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}^{(H)}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D}(A)}\left\|{\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|^{2}}.

(134)

Now, we have
\langle\psi_{D}|\psi_{D}\rangle=\frac{1}{|\mathbf{D}(A)|}
. Furthermore, observe that the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{KF}}
registers, i.e.
Equation

131
, will have a single set of input-output registers which participate in the chain from
u
to
v
. If
D_{k}[u_{L}\rightarrow z^{\prime}]
is re-assigned to
z^{\prime}\neq z
, then this chain will not begin at
u
, rather at
u_{L}\|(u_{R}\oplus z\oplus z^{\prime})
. Hence, there is a record of
z
external to
D_{k}(u_{L})
. We can therefore apply
Lemma

2.9
, obtaining

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot(2^{n}-t)}\right),

(135)

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G^{(H)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}-t}}\right).

(136)

∎

Lemma 4.24
.

Fix some
A\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
and
u\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(A)
which
A
allows, to write the state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\alpha_{v}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(137)

Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}^{(K)}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}-t}}\right).

(138)

Proof.

This proof proceeds similarly to that of
Lemma

4.23
.
∎

Lemma 4.25
.

Fix some
A\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
and
u\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(A)
which
A
allows, to write the state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\alpha_{v}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(139)

We take
\alpha_{v}=0
for
v\not\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}^{(F)}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{T}{2^{n}-t}}\right),

(140)

where
T=\sum_{l\in\{0,1\}^{n}}T_{l}
, and
T_{l}=\left|\sum_{r\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\alpha_{l\|r}\right|^{2}
.

Proof.

We will use the same setup as the proof for
Lemma

4.23
. Let us consider some
D\in\mathbf{D}(A)
, and take
|A|=t
. We can write the component of
\ket{\psi}
which is an extension of this state as

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{E}^{3}(D))}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

(141)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot(2^{n}-t)}}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{u_{R}\oplus\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\}}\ket{D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\otimes

(142)

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\alpha_{v}\ket{D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus z\rightarrow u_{L}\oplus v_{L}],D_{f}[v_{L}\rightarrow u_{R}\oplus z\oplus v_{R}]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{KF}}}.

(143)

Further, recall from
Lemma

4.14
that these states are orthogonal and span the relevant space, so we can write

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D}(A)}\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}},

(144)

so by linearity it suffices to analyze each component separately. Let us define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{D}=\bigcup_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}}\mathcal{H}(D[u\rightarrow v]),

(145)

where we have
\mathcal{H}_{D}\perp\mathcal{H}_{D^{\prime}}
for
D\neq D^{\prime}\in\mathbf{D}(A)
from
Corollaries

4.20
and
4.18
. Observe that
\ket{\psi_{D}}\in\mathcal{H}_{D}
, and furthermore the image under
G^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}
will remain in this space. Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}^{(F)}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{D\in\mathbf{D}(A)}\left\|{\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-G^{(F)}_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|^{2}}.

(146)

We can analyze the action of
G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}^{(F)}
on this state as

\displaystyle G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}^{(F)}\ket{\psi_{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot(2^{n}-t)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{u_{R}\oplus\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\}\\
l\in\mathbf{L}_{u,A}\end{subarray}}\ket{D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z],D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus z\rightarrow u_{L}\oplus l]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{fC}_{l,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}\sum_{r\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\alpha_{l\|r}\ket{D_{f}[l\rightarrow u_{R}\oplus z\oplus r]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}},

(147)

from which it is clear to see that

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi_{D}}-G^{(F)}_{u}\ket{\psi_{D}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot(2^{n}-t)}}\cdot

(148)

\displaystyle\quad\sqrt{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{u_{R}\oplus\mathsf{Dom}(D_{k})\}\\
l\in\mathbf{L}_{u,A}\end{subarray}}\left\|{\sum_{r\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left(\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}-\mathsf{fC}_{l,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}\right)\alpha_{l\|r}\ket{D_{f}[l\rightarrow u_{R}\oplus z\oplus r]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}}\right\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot(2^{n}-t)}}\cdot O\left(\sqrt{\sum_{l\in\mathbf{L}_{u,A}}\left|\sum_{r\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\alpha_{l\|r}\right|^{2}}\right)

(149)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{T}{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot(2^{n}-t)}}\right),

(150)

which combined with
Equation

146
shows the claim.
∎

4.4
Cromulent twirling

We now turn to working out properties of the twirling permutations. A number of statistical properties are required of the distribution for our analysis to hold. First, define the left and right action of permutations on databases as

\displaystyle\pi*I

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\pi(x),y)\,:\,(x,y)\in I\}

(151)

\displaystyle I*\omega

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(x,\omega^{-1}(y))\,:(x,y)\in I\}.

(152)

This action corresponds to the internal database which is supported; if the full masked Feistel ensemble supports
I
, then the internal three rounds of Feistel will support
\pi*I*\omega
. In this way, we will require certain properties of
\pi
and
\omega
to ensure that this internal database is allowable, as well as to understand the action of a query.

Definition 4.26
.

We say
\pi,\omega
resolve
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
if
\pi*I*\omega\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
. We let
\mathbf{R}_{I}
be the set of permutation pairs
(\pi,\omega)
which resolve
I
. For some fixed
A\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
, we let
\mathbf{R}_{I\rightarrow A}
be the set of permutation pairs
(\pi,\omega)
such that
\pi*I*\omega=A
. For some fixed
x\in[N]
, we let
\mathbf{R}_{x,I}
be the set of
\pi,\omega
which resolve
I
and for which
\pi*I*\omega\heartsuit\pi(x)
, meaning the internal database allows
\pi(x)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution on
\mathbf{S}_{N}^{\times 2}
, and
p_{\mathcal{D}}
be the corresponding probability function. We use the notation
\mathcal{D}_{I}
to denote the induced distribution of
\mathcal{D}
conditioned on resolving
I
. In other words,

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{D}_{I}}(\pi,\omega)

\displaystyle=p_{\mathcal{D}}(\pi,\omega\,|\,(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{I}).

(153)

Similarly,
\mathcal{D}_{I\rightarrow A}
for
A\in\mathbf{A}
is the distribution conditioned on satisfying
\pi*I*\omega=A
, and
\mathcal{D}_{x,I}
is the distribution conditioned on the inner distribution allowing
\pi(x)
. Formally,

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{D}_{I\rightarrow A}}(\pi,\omega)

\displaystyle=p_{\mathcal{D}}(\pi,\omega\,|\,(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{I\rightarrow A}),

(154)

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)

\displaystyle=p_{\mathcal{D}}(\pi,\omega\,|\,(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}).

(155)

We say that such a distribution is
cromulent
if it satisfies the following three properties. These are statistical properties about the distribution which will suffice to show that the twirl hides the internal three Feistel rounds.

Definition 4.27
.

Distribution
\mathcal{D}
is
cromulent
if, for all
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
,
x\neq x^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)
,
l\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and
y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{I}}[(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}]

\displaystyle=1-O(t^{2}/2^{n}),

(156)

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}},

(157)

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{3n}}\right),

(158)

and for all
(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}
and
A=\pi*I*\omega
we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}}(\pi,\omega)}{N-t}}-\sqrt{\frac{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}{|\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),A}|}}

\displaystyle=\pm O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}{|\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),A}|}}\right)

(159)

Further, all equations hold in the inverse direction, if we replace
(\pi,\omega)
with
(\omega^{-1},\pi^{-1})
,
I
with
I^{-1}
,
x
with
y
and
x^{\prime}
with
y^{\prime}
.

The first condition captures the intuition that each new query should, with high probability, lead to a non-colliding left wire at the start of the internal Feistel. The second and third point capture properties necessary for compression to work as desired: compression should only remove a chain when the algorithm uncomputes an input-output pair. For plain three-round Feistel, uncomputing only the last
n
bits of an input would suffice to remove the last element of its chain. These conditions capture that the last
n
bits are sufficiently scrambled by
\omega
to render this attack, and anything like it, impossible. The last equation is a technical requirement related to normalization, and is due to small deviations in the number of allowable databases.

Note that the final remark concerning inverses is implied by the previous equations if
\mathcal{D}
is invariant under the flip operation,
(\pi,\omega)\mapsto(\omega^{-1},\pi^{-1})
. In all of the settings we consider, this will be the case. It therefore suffices to show the properties for the listed cases only.
The following lemmas establish that the uniform distribution
p_{\mathcal{D}}(\pi,\omega)=1/(N!)^{2}
is cromulent. We will in turn show that, so long as the twirling permutations are cromulent, the masked Feistel ensemble is indistinguishable from our compressed permutation oracle.

Lemma 4.28
.

Fix some
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
and
x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathbf{R}_{I}}[\pi*I*\omega\text{ allows }\pi(x)]

\displaystyle=1-O(t^{2}/2^{n}).

(160)

Proof.

Consider sampling all values
\pi(x)
for
x\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)
, and all values of
\omega
such that
\pi*I*\omega\in\mathbf{A}_{t}
. The remaining values of
\pi
may be sampled uniformly at random, and so
\pi(x)
comes from a set of size
2^{2n}-t
. There are
O(t^{2}2^{n})
values which are not allowed (from the
O(t^{2})
left substrings times
2^{n}
right substrings), so we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathbf{R}_{I}}[\pi*I*\omega\text{ allows }\pi(x)]

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{2n}-O(t^{2}2^{n})}{2^{2n}-t}

(161)

\displaystyle=1-O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right).

(162)

∎

Lemma 4.29
.

Fix some
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
, some
x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)
, and
l\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Then, for all
y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\sim\mathbf{R}_{x,I}\end{subarray}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}},

(163)

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\sim\mathbf{R}_{x,I}\end{subarray}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{3n}}\right).

(164)

Proof.

For the first equation, observe that allowability in this sense is translation invariant on the internal left wire: if
\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{I}
, then we can construct
\omega^{\prime}(v)=\omega(v\oplus(s\|0^{n}))
and
\pi^{\prime}(x)=\pi(x)\oplus(s\|0^{n})
. Then, for any
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
the newly constructed permutations will also be in
\mathbf{R}_{I}
. Therefore, the distribution induced by
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}
is uniform.

For the second equation, let us analyze the probability that that
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}
. Consider fixing all of the other input-output points of
\pi
and
\omega
such that the internal database is allowable. Then, both
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}
and
\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}
are chosen uniformly at random from a universe of size
2^{2n}-t
. Therefore, there will be a collision on the left wire with probability
2^{-n}\pm O(t2^{-2n})
. Combining this with the previous translation invariance, observe that we can randomly shift the constructed
\pi,\omega
as described above after this procedure. This gives a
2^{-n}
probability that
\omega(y)=l
, independent of the prior event, leaving us with the result

\displaystyle\Pr_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\sim\mathbf{R}_{x,I}\end{subarray}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\pm O\left(\frac{t}{2^{3n}}\right).

(165)

This is tighter than the bound stated, showing the claim.
∎

Lemma 4.30
.

For any
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
,
x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)
,
y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)
, and
\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}
with
A=\pi*I*\omega
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{R}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}|\cdot(N-t)}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{R}_{x,I}|\cdot|\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),A}|}}

\displaystyle=\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}\sqrt{|\mathbf{R}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}|\cdot(N-t)}}\right).

(166)

Proof.

Observe that we have

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathbf{R}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}|}{|\mathbf{R}_{x,I}|}

\displaystyle=1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{N-t}{|\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),\mathbf{A}}|}

\displaystyle=1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right),

(167)

the first by an argument analogous to
Lemma

4.28
and the second by
Lemma

4.10
. If we let
a=|\mathbf{R}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}|\cdot(N-t)
, then we can write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{R}_{x,I}|\cdot|\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),A}|}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a(1\pm O(t^{2}/2^{n})}}

(168)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}\cdot\sqrt{a}}\right),

(169)

where the last line follows from the taylor expansion of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{a+x}}
about
x=0
.
∎

Going forward, we will say that
P=(\pi,\omega,D)
supports
I
if
D
supports
\pi*I*\omega
.

5
Soundness

In this section, we will establish that our compressed permutation oracle is indistinguishable from a random permutation oracle. We will do this by showing that an oracle for the masked Feistel construction with uniform twirl (which is therefore a uniform permutation oracle) is indistinguishable from our compressed permutation oracle.

In fact, all of the machinery we develop prior to the main theorem will assume only that the masked Feistel has a cromulent twirling distributions. We take
\mathcal{D}
to be the distribution on
\mathbf{S}_{N}^{\times 2}
from which the twirling permutations are drawn. The only part of this section where we assume that
\mathcal{D}
is uniform is in the main
Theorem

5.19
. In
Section

6
, we will use a different cromulent distribution to establish the quantum security of the Feistel construction, re-using much of the machinery developed here.

5.1
Valid, elegant, and sophisticated subspaces

We will begin with the
sophisticated
subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
, which will capture purifications corresponding to compressed permutation databases. Towards this, we first define the notation
\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}
for
I\in\mathbf{I}
, where dependence on
\mathcal{D}
is suppressed, as

\displaystyle\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{I}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(\pi*I*\omega)}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(170)

Observe that these states satisfy
\langle\mathbf{P}(I)|\mathbf{P}(I^{\prime})\rangle=\delta_{I,I^{\prime}}
, as for any
I\neq I^{\prime}
the two states have distinct supports. The span of these states form a subspace, which we call the
sophisticated
subspace, and refer to by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{soph}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{span}\{\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}\,:\,I\in\mathbf{I}_{\leq 2^{n}}\},

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{I\in\mathbf{I}_{\leq 2^{n}}}|\mathbf{P}(I)\rangle\langle\mathbf{P}(I)|.

(171)

Then, we can takes these states to define our intertwining isometry.

Definition 5.1
.

We define the
intertwiner

\mathcal{I}:\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})\oplus\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})
as the isometry

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\mathbb{1}+\sum_{I\in\mathbf{I}_{\leq 2^{n}}}|I\rangle\langle\mathbf{P}(I)|-|\mathbf{P}(I)\rangle\langle\mathbf{P}(I)|.

(172)

In other words,
\mathcal{I}
is an isometry which maps
\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}
to
\ket{I}
, and is the identity on all states in
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}
.

As a technical note, we will take the compressed permutation oracle purifying register to be
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})\oplus\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})
in this section. We will consider the permutation compression operator
\mathsf{pC}
, the purified query operation
\mathsf{P}
, and flip operation
\mathsf{F}
, to act as the identity on the
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})
component. We will still label this entire Hilbert space as register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}
, with the expectation that only a small portion of the state will lie in the
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})
component. Observe that all of the aforementioned operators (and therefore
\mathsf{cpO}
) preserve the two subspaces, in that they can be written as a direct sum
O_{1}\oplus O_{2}
for operator
O_{1}
on
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
and
O_{2}
on
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})
. Therefore, the compressed oracle experiment with this extended purification is perfectly indistinguishable from the standard compressed oracle experiment.
11
11

11

The reason we do this instead of constructing the isometry from
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
to
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})
is that it is unclear how to define validity for the compressed permutation oracle. In the isometric subspace, we will see that it is easy to define, which in turn will be used to conclude validity of our construction.

Let us observe the simple fact that
\mathcal{I}
is an intertwiner between the flip operators
\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{mfF}
on the sophisticated subspace, as well as between the purified oracles
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{mfP}
. Observe that the the sophisticated subspace is maintained by the masked Feistel flip operator
\mathsf{mfF}
and masked Feistel purified query operator
\mathsf{mfP}
.

Remark 5.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}},

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(173)

Proof.

For the first equation, observe that if
P=(\pi,\omega,D_{h},D_{k},D_{f})\in\mathbf{P}
supports injective database
I
, then the flipped
(\omega^{-1},\pi^{-1},D_{f},D_{k},D_{h})
supports
I^{-1}
. This holds even outside the sophisticated subspace, so it holds on it as well.

For the second equation, recall that
\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
sends
\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}
to
\ket{I}
, which determines it’s action entirely on the sophisticated subspace. The support of every standard-basis state in the sum defining
\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}
is
I
, and so
\mathsf{mfP}
answers queries identically to
\mathsf{P}
.
∎

We will define the
valid
subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}=\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})
as the span of states where the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}
registers lie purely in the valid subspace as defined in
Section

2.3
. We will use the notation
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{val}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
to denote this subspace, and
\Pi^{\mathsf{val}}
to denote the projector onto it, which can be defined as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathsf{val}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Xi_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\Xi_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}\Xi_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}.

(174)

From
Lemma

2.7
, queries to the internal functions preserves this subspace. It follows that
\mathsf{cmfO}
preserves it as well, meaning
\mathsf{cmfO}
can be written of the form
\mathsf{cmfO}=O_{1}\oplus O_{2}
where
O_{1}:\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{val}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{val}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
and
O_{2}:\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{val},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{val},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
are unitary operators.

A related subspace is the
query valid
subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{qval}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
, this time a subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
. The projector onto this subspace is denoted
\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}
, and the subspace captures the property that, after decompression, the query point will have a well-defined output. To define this, we will first extend the definition of the decompressed projector
\Gamma_{x}
to
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{P})
, writing

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}0&\text{(If $\mathsf{Supp}(P)(x)=\bot$)}\\
\ket{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(175)

\displaystyle\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\Gamma_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(176)

We also need the notation
\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
denote the
\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
operator controlled on the
b
bit of the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
register, which can be written

\displaystyle\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{b,x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}

\displaystyle=\ket{b,x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\begin{cases}\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}&\text{(If $b=0$)}\\
\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.&\text{(If $b=1$)}\end{cases}

(177)

With this in hand, we can define the query valid projector as

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}\cdot\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}^{\dagger})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}

(178)

Observe that
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{qval}}\leq\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{val}}
, or in words, if the purification register is valid then decompressing on any query point will result in a well defined answer. This has the simple corollary that every state reached by an algorithm lies in the query valid subspace.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a quantum algorithm making
q
queries with intermediate operations
A_{0},\dots,A_{q}
, and define

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXY}}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXY}}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXY}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXY}}}\ket{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(179)

Then we have
\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{val}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
, and thus
\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
.

Note that the projectors onto valid or query valid do not commute with projectors onto the sophisticated subspace. We would like to focus our attention on states which are both query valid and sophisticated, as these are the ones which we will have tools to analyze. Unfortunately, given the preceding fact, it is difficult to understand this intersection space.

Instead, we will work with a subspace of the sophisticated space which we call the
elegant
subspace, denoted
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{ele}}
. This space will act as a proxy for the intersection of sophisticated and query valid. It is a subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{soph}}
. We write
\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}
for the projector onto
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{ele}}
, and can define for any
b\in\{0,1\},x\in[N]

\displaystyle\ket{+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|I|}}\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(I[x\rightarrow y])}},

(180)

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{b,x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{b,x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\sum_{I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}\,:\,|I|<2^{m}}\left(\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}-|+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}\rangle\langle+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}|\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

(181)

To understand this definition, consider a state of the form

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}=\ket{0,x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\left(\alpha_{\bot}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}+\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}\alpha_{y}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(I[x\rightarrow y])}}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}},

(182)

for some
x\in[N]
and
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
of size less then
2^{n}
. This state will lie in the elegant subspace if and only if
\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}\alpha_{y}=0
. If we replace the
\mathbf{P}(I)
and
{\mathbf{P}(I[x\rightarrow y])}
with
I
and
I[x\rightarrow y]
above (considering a purification register
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
), then this is precisely the condition that we will obtain a valid output after applying decompression
\mathsf{pC}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
. In this way, the elegant subspace captures states which, after applying the intertwiner
\mathcal{I}
, will have a query valid answer on decompression
\mathsf{pC}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
.

Note also that we do not consider the value of
b
in the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
register; this subspace will be most relevant when the decompression operator is applied during the compressed masked Feistel. In this scenario, the database will have already been flipped in the case
b=1
. As a result, this subspace is defined assuming a forwards query.
It will also be worth defining a version of the elegant subspace conjugated by the controlled flip, called
flip elegant
. We will define it by its projector. Intuitively, this projector capture the same structure as the elegant projector; the state before applying
\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}
will be flip elegant (i.e. the input state to
\mathsf{cmfO}
), and the state after applying the
\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}
will be elegant (i.e. the input state to decompression
\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}
). We write

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}^{\dagger})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}.

(183)

While this subspace is not exactly the same as the intersection of sophisticated and query valid, it can act as a proxy. We can formalize this using the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{soph}}
be a sophisticated quantum state satisfying
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
, and supported on purifications of size at most
t
. If we define

\displaystyle\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}},

\displaystyle\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}},

(184)

then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\phi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}-\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(185)

We defer the proof of this lemma until we have more technical machinery.

5.2
A twirling lemma

The following lemma will be key in analyzing masked Feistel. It allows us to formalize the intuition that the twirling permutations hide the internal Feistel structure. For intuition, consider the lemma statement without the condition
\sum_{y\in[N]}\alpha_{y}=0
. Then, we could set
\alpha_{y}=2^{-n}
, and we would obtain
\mathbb{E}_{\omega\sim D}[T]=2^{n}
. What we show is that, for any orthogonal vector, this quantity is comparatively small. This, in turn, will allow us to show that the masked Feistel compression operator is indeed close to the identity on the subspace where the corresponding compressed permutation oracle acts as the identity.

Lemma 5.5
.

Fix some vector
\ket{\alpha}=\sum_{y\in[N]}\alpha_{y}\ket{y}
of unit norm, and such that
\sum_{y\in[N]}\alpha_{y}=0
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution such that
for all
y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]
with
\alpha_{y}\neq 0
and
\alpha_{y^{\prime}}\neq 0
, and
l\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=2^{-n},

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=2^{-2n}\pm O(t^{2}2^{-3n}).

(186)

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}}[T]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}}\left[\sum_{l\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left|\sum_{r\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\alpha_{\omega(l\|r)}\right|^{2}\right]=O(t^{2}).

(187)

Proof.

Let us compute the expectation of
T_{l}
, defined as

\displaystyle T_{l}

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{r\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\alpha_{\omega(l\|r)}\right|^{2}.

(188)

We can write
I_{y}
to be the indicator variable that is
1
if
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=l
. If
\alpha_{y}=0
, then we take
I_{y}
to be
1
with probability
2^{-n}
independent of the other
I_{y^{\prime}}
, which we can do without changing the following equation. Then we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[T_{l}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\sum_{y\in[N]}I_{y}\alpha_{y}\right|^{2}\right]

(189)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{y\in[N]}I_{y}^{2}\left|\alpha_{y}\right|^{2}+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]}I_{y}I_{y^{\prime}}\left|\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}\right|\right]

(190)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[N]}\mathbb{E}[I_{y}]\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y}^{*}+\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]}\mathbb{E}[I_{y}I_{y^{\prime}}]\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}^{*}

(191)

\displaystyle=2^{-n}\underbrace{\sum_{y\in[N]}\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y}^{*}}_{S_{1}}+2^{-2n}\underbrace{\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]}\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}^{*}}_{S_{2}}+\underbrace{\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]}\pm O(t^{2}2^{-3n})\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}^{*}}_{S_{3}},

(192)

where the last line follows from cromulence of
\mathcal{D}
.
It is clear that
S_{1}=1
. We can compute
S_{2}
as

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]}\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}^{*}

(193)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[N]}\alpha_{y}\left(\sum_{y^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\{y\}}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}^{*}\right)

(194)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in[N]}-\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y}^{*}

(By
\sum_{y\in[N]}\alpha_{y}=0
)

(195)

\displaystyle=-1.

(196)

It remains to compute
S_{3}
. Note that we cannot factor out the
O(t^{2}2^{-3n})
, as the constant pre-factor and sign may be different for different values of
y
and
y^{\prime}
. Instead we apply triangle inequality, obtaining

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]}\pm O(t^{2}2^{-3n})\alpha_{y}\alpha_{y^{\prime}}^{*}

(197)

\displaystyle\leq O(t^{2}2^{-3n})\sum_{y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]}|\alpha_{y}||\alpha_{y^{\prime}}^{*}|

(Triangle inequality)

(198)

\displaystyle\leq O(t^{2}2^{-3n})\left(\sum_{y\in[N]}|\alpha_{y}|\right)^{2}

(199)

\displaystyle\leq O(t^{2}2^{-n}).

(200)

We therefore have
0\leq\mathbb{E}[T_{l}]=O(t^{2}2^{-n})
for all
l\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and so the claim follows by linearity of expectation, and the identity
T=\sum_{l\in\{0,1\}^{n}}T_{l}
.
∎

5.3
Sanitized and ideal masked Feistel

We will here define two modification to the masked Feistel oracle, called the
ideal
masked Feistel denoted
\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}
, and the
sanitized
masked Feistel denoted
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}
, both of which will be helpful in our analysis. For this section, we will imagine the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
register to contain only the query point
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
, i.e. the forward query case. Recall that we answer inverse queries by flipping the truth table, answering as a forward query, and flipping back. In this way, due to the symmetry of both masked Feistel and our compressed permutation oracle, it will suffice to analyze only forwards queries here.

Let us begin by defining the ideal masked Feistel compression operator
\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}
. This operator is the identity on the non-elegant subspace. On the elegant subspace, we will first define it for some fixed
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
of size
|I|<2^{n}
, defining
\mathsf{mfC}_{x,I}
as an operator on
\mathcal{H}(\{\mathbf{P}(I^{\prime})\,:\,I^{\prime}\in I|^{x}\})
.

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x,I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{1}-|\mathbf{P}(I)\rangle\langle\mathbf{P}(I)|-|+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}\rangle\langle+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}|+|\mathbf{P}(I)\rangle\langle+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}|+|+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}\rangle\langle\mathbf{P}(I)|

(201)

The masked Feistel decompression and its coherently controlled counterpart are then naturally defined as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigoplus_{I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x},|I|<2^{n}}\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x,I,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(202)

Note the clear similarity to the definition of the permutation compression operator
\mathsf{pC}
; the only difference being the use of
\mathbf{P}(I)
in place of
I
, reflecting the distinct—but isomorphic—Hilbert spaces on which these operators act. This can be formalized via the following two remarks.

Remark 5.6
.

The operator
\mathcal{I}
intertwines ideal masked Feistel decompression
\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}
on the elegant subspace with decompression
\mathsf{pC}^{\dagger}
on the corresponding subspace.

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}=\mathsf{pC}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

(203)

Remark 5.7
.

The action of
\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
interchanges the elegant space and the intersection of
\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}
and the sophisticated space,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}=\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}.

(204)

We use this to define the ideal masked feistel oracle
\overline{\mathsf{cmfO}}
, which acts as

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{cmfO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}.

(205)

The following remark is based on similar reasoning to the prior.

Remark 5.8
.

The ideal compressed mask Feistel operator is exactly intertwined by
\mathcal{I}
with the compressed permutation oracle, on the flip elegant subspace.

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{cmfO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(206)

The ideal operator is what we are aiming for, in that its action is precisely analogous to the compressed permutation oracle up to a change of basis. To show that the action of the ideal
\overline{\mathsf{cmfO}}
is close the actual
\mathsf{cmfO}
, we will define the sanitized compression as an intermediate operation. This operator is similar to standard compression, except it will maintain canonicity of the internal
D_{h},D_{k},D_{f}
tuple of databases. We define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\pi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{\pi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes G_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}.

(207)

Observe that this operator is close the masked Feistel compression operator.

Corollary 5.9

(of
Lemma

4.22
)
.

The sanitized and unsanitized masked Feistel compression operators are close:
\left\|{\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}-\mathsf{mfC}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)
.

We will therefore be able to replace the unsanitized
\mathsf{mfC}
with its sanitized variant
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}
.
It will further be helpful to define the
allowability
projector
\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
. This projects onto the subspace in which the query point
x
is allowed by the purification, i.e.

\displaystyle\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi,\omega,D\,:\,D\heartsuit\pi(x)}|\pi,\omega,D\rangle\langle\pi,\omega,D|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(208)

Naturally, the coherently controlled version is defined as
\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}=\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
. A useful fact about this projector is that it acts gently on the sophisticated subspace when the input point is undefined, which follows from the cromulence of
\mathcal{D}
.

Lemma 5.10
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|{(\mathbb{1}-\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{x})\Pi^{x\in\mathsf{db},\perp}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(209)

Proof.

First, let us consider some fixed
I\in\mathbf{I}_{\leq 2^{n}}
where
x\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)
(any state orthogonal to this family is annihilated by
\Pi^{x\in\mathsf{db},\perp}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}
). We can write

\displaystyle\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{I}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{D}(\pi*I*\omega)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}},

(210)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}-\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{I},\\
(\pi,\omega)\not\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}\end{subarray}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes\ket{\mathbf{D}(\pi*I*\omega)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}}\right\|

(211)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{I}}[(\pi,\omega)\not\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}]}

(212)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right),\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{(By cromulence)}

(213)

in particular the property established in
Lemma

4.28
.
Further, observe that the state in
Equation

211
is supported entirely on purifications
P
with the property
\mathsf{Supp}(P)=I
. Therefore, if we had considered a different database
I
, we would have an orthogonal state. The claim then follows from
Lemma

2.3
.
∎

Now, we will show closeness of ideal and real in the case where the purification does not support the input point.

Lemma 5.11
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|{(\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db},\perp}\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(214)

Proof.

We begin with a simpler claim.

Claim
.

Let
x\in[N]
and
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
be of size
t=|I|<2^{n}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{x}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}-\ket{+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(215)

Further,
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x}\cdot\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{x}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}
is supported entirely on the span of
\ket{\mathbf{P}(I[x\rightarrow y])}
for
y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)
.

To prove this claim, observe that the state after the projection is

\displaystyle\Pi^{\heartsuit}_{x}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\mathbf{D}(\pi*I*\omega)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\otimes\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}

(216)

\displaystyle=\left(1-O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right)\right)\cdot\sum_{(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\mathbf{D}(\pi*I*\omega)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\otimes\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}},

(217)

by cromulence.
Applying the
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x}

\displaystyle\cdot\Pi_{\heartsuit x}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}=\left(1-O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right)\right)\cdot\sum_{(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\left(G_{\pi(x)}^{\dagger}\ket{\mathbf{D}(\pi*I*\omega)}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\otimes\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}

(218)

\displaystyle=\left(1-O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right)\right)\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I},\\
A\coloneqq\pi*I*\omega\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{v\in\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),A}}\sqrt{\frac{{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}}{{|\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),A}|}}}\ket{\mathbf{D}(A[\pi(x)\rightarrow v])}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\otimes\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}

(219)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I),\\
(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{I[x\rightarrow y]},\\
A\coloneqq\pi*I*\omega\end{subarray}}\left(1\pm O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)\right)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}}(\pi,\omega)}}{{N-t}}}\cdot\ket{\mathbf{D}(A[\pi(x)\rightarrow\omega^{-1}(y)])}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\otimes\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}},

(220)

where the second line follows from
Lemma

4.22
, and the last line from cromulence, in particular the property established in
Lemma

4.30
.
Observe that the above state would, without the
1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right)
term, be precisely
\ket{+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}}
. The two claims follow.

With this in hand, recall that any state orthogonal to
\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}
for
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
will be annihilated by
\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db},\perp}\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}
. Further, by definition we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ket{+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}}.

(221)

Thus, for any state in the family
\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
, we have that the images of
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}_{x}
and of
\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}
lie in the span of
\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I[x\rightarrow y])}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
. We would obtain an orthogonal subspace for any different choice if
x\in[N]
and
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
, so these operators are close for any state in the linear combination. Putting everything together, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{(\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}P}\cdot(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db},\perp}\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}\leq\underbrace{\left\|{(\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}P}\cdot(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db},\perp}\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}}_{O(\sqrt{t^{2}/2^{n}})}+

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\underbrace{\left\|{(\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}\Pi^{\heartsuit}-\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}P}\cdot(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db},\perp}\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}}_{O(\sqrt{t^{2}/2^{n}})}+\underbrace{\left\|{(\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}-\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}\Pi^{\heartsuit})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}P}\cdot(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db},\perp}\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}}_{O(\sqrt{t^{2}/2^{n}})}

(222)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(223)

∎

Another important case is the portion of the elegant subspace where the query point is already defined prior to decompression.

Lemma 5.12
.

We have
