\displaystyle\left\|{(\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(224)

Proof.

Let
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{ele}}
be an elegant quantum state, where the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}
register is supported on databases of size at most
t
, and the query point is defined:
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\in\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
.
Note that the entire operation is controlled on the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
register, so by
Lemma

2.3
it suffices to do the analysis for some fixed
x\in[N]
. Further, we can replace
\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}
by
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}
by
Corollary

5.9
. The action of ideal decompression
\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}
on this subspace is the identity, which we would like to show is close the action of
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}
. We will drop the dagger
†
from
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}
, observing that if
U^{\dagger}
is close to
\mathbb{1}
on some subspace then by linearity so is
U
. In other words, we will show for any fixed
x\in[N]
that
\left\|{\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{x}\ket{\psi}-\ket{\psi}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)
for any elegant
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{ele}}
with
\ket{\psi}\in\Pi^{x\in\mathsf{db}}
.

Let us expand
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}
into it’s constituent parts. We will begin with the
G^{(F)}
application, considering

\displaystyle\left\|{\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}|\pi\rangle\langle\pi|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes G^{(F)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\right)\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}}\right\|.

(225)

Observe that both states (the one after applying the left or the right operator in the above parenthesis) will be supported entirely on purifications
P
such that
\mathsf{Supp}(P)|^{x}=I|^{x}
. This is because a re-assignment to the final element of the chain beginning at
\pi(x)
(in
D_{f}
) can only modify the chain beginning at
\pi(x)
.
Let us make then the assumption that the state
\ket{\psi}
is supported purely on states of the form
\ket{\mathbf{P}(I[x\rightarrow y])}
for
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
of size
|I|=t-1
. The other components of
\ket{\psi}
will remain orthogonal, so by
Lemma

2.3
it suffices to do the analysis on this component only. We will write

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}=\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}\alpha_{y}\ket{\mathbf{P}(I[x\rightarrow y]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}},

(226)

where
\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}\alpha_{y}=0
. We can equivalently write

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I},\\
A\coloneqq\pi*I*\omega,\\
u\coloneqq\pi(x)\end{subarray}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{\omega(v)}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\right).

(227)

Applying the
G^{(F)}
operator, we find

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}|\pi\rangle\langle\pi|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes G^{(F)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\right)\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I},\\
A\coloneqq\pi*I*\omega,\\
u\coloneqq\pi(x)\end{subarray}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\quad\left(G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}^{(F)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{\omega(v)}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\right),

(228)

giving us difference

\displaystyle\left\|{\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}|\pi\rangle\langle\pi|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes G^{(F)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\right)\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\Bigg\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I},\\
A\coloneqq\pi*I*\omega,\\
u\coloneqq\pi(x)\end{subarray}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\quad\left(\left(G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}^{(F)}-\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{\omega(v)}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\right)\Bigg\|

(229)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}}{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\cdot T_{\omega,\alpha}}

(230)

where
T_{\omega,\alpha}=\sum_{l\in\{0,1\}^{n}}T_{l,\omega,\alpha}
and
T_{l,\omega,\alpha}=\left(\sum_{r\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left|\alpha_{\omega(l\|r)}\right|\right)^{2}
. This last line follows from
Lemma

4.25
. This can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\left\|{\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}|\pi\rangle\langle\pi|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes G^{(F)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}-\mathbb{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\right)\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}\left[\frac{T_{\omega,\alpha}}{2^{n}-t}\right]}

(231)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right),

(232)

where the last line follows from
Lemma

5.5
combined with cromulence, and in particular
Lemma

4.29
. Therefore, we may replace the application of
G^{(F)}
in
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}
with the identity
\mathbb{1}
at the above cost. A similar argument shows that the application of
G^{(K)}
and
G^{(H)}
can be replaced with the identity
\mathbb{1}
at a cost
O(\sqrt{t^{2}/2^{n}})
, using
Lemma

4.24
and
Lemma

4.23
repsectively in place of
Lemma

4.25
. Indeed, for these cases the final step corresponding to
Equations

231
and
232
can be ommitted, due to the simpler form of
Lemmas

4.23
and
4.24
.
∎

Corollary 5.13

(of
Lemmas

5.11
and
5.12
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|{(\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}-\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger})\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right),

(233)

\displaystyle\left\|{(\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}-\mathsf{mfC})\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(234)

Proof.

The first equation is immediate from the referenced lemmas.
To conclude the second, by
Corollary

5.9
we may substitute
\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}
for
\mathsf{mfC}
. Then, observe that

\displaystyle\left\|{(\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}-\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}})\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=\left\|{(\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}-\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}})\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

(235)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}-\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}}\right\|_{\leq t}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(236)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(237)

∎

We can now prove the main result for this section, establishing the soundness of our intertwiner on the elegant subspace.

Lemma 5.14
.

On the flip elegant subspace,
\mathcal{I}
is an approximate intertwiner between the compressed permutation oracle and the compressed masked Feistel oracle,

\displaystyle\left\|{(\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}-\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}})\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(238)

Proof.

We can write

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}=\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}

(239)

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt=\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}

(240)

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt=\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(
Corollary

5.13
)

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt=\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(
Remark

5.7
)

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt=\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot(\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(
Corollary

5.13
)

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt=\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfP}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{ctrl\mathchar 45\relax}\mathsf{mfF}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-36.135pt=\overline{\mathsf{cmfO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(241)

The claim now follows from
Remark

5.8
.
∎

The final case is decompression on states which are sophisticated but not elegant. For a technical reason, we will show that the decompression operator results in an invalid output and non-sophisticated purification. This will be useful in bounding the component of the state on this subspace. We use
\mathcal{H}_{A}/\mathcal{H}_{B}
for spaces
\mathcal{H}_{B}\leq\mathcal{H}_{A}
to denote the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{H}_{B}
in
\mathcal{H}_{A}
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{soph}}/\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{ele}})
be a sophisticated, but not elegant, quantum state supported on purifications of size at most
t
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\right),

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\right).

(242)

Proof.

Observe that that
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\in\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
, the orthogonal complement of
\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
in the sophisticated space
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{soph}}
lies in the elegant subspace
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{ele}}
. By definition, we can write the sanitized masked Feistel decompression as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}|\pi\rangle\langle\pi|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes\left(G^{(F)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}G^{(K)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}G^{(H)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HK}}}\right),

(243)

where we recall that the
G^{(H)},G^{(K)},G^{(F)}
unitaries are involutions. By an argument analogous to that given in the proof of
Lemma

5.12
, the first two operations applied to the state,
G^{(H)},G^{(K)}
, perturb the state a distance at most
O(\sqrt{1/2^{n}})
. By triangle inequality, we may therefore consider only the action of of the final
G^{(F)}
operator. For now, let us fix some
x\in[N]
to be the value in the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
register, and
I\in\mathbf{I}|^{x}
of size
|I|=t-1
, and work out the action on the
\ket{+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
state. We find

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}|\pi\rangle\langle\pi|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes G^{(F)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\right)\ket{+_{x,\mathbf{P}(I)}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I},\\
A\coloneqq\pi*I*\omega,\\
u\coloneqq\pi(x)\end{subarray}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\quad\left(G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}^{(F)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathbf{V}_{u,A}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{V}_{u,A}|}}\ket{+_{\mathbf{D}(A[u\rightarrow v])}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\right)

(244)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I},\\
A\coloneqq\pi*I*\omega,\\
u\coloneqq\pi(x)\end{subarray}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\quad G_{u,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}^{(F)}\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\in\mathbf{D}(A),\\
z\in H(u,D),\\
w\in K(u,D),\\
r\in\{0,1\}^{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot|H(u,D)|\cdot|K(u,D)|\cdot 2^{n}}}\cdot

\displaystyle\quad\ket{D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus z\rightarrow w]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}\ket{D_{f}[u_{L}\oplus w\rightarrow r]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}\Bigg)

(245)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I},\\
A\coloneqq\pi*I*\omega,\\
u\coloneqq\pi(x)\end{subarray}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega}}}\otimes

\displaystyle\quad\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\in\mathbf{D}(A),\\
z\in H(u,D),\\
w\in K(u,D)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbf{D}(A)|\cdot|H(u,D)|\cdot|K(u,D)|}}\cdot

\displaystyle\quad\ket{D_{h}[u_{L}\rightarrow z]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{D_{k}[u_{R}\oplus z\rightarrow w]}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{K}}}\ket{D_{f}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{F}}}\Bigg).

(246)

The key properties of this state is that it is fully undefined on input
x
, as can be seen by the lack of assignment of
u_{L}\oplus w
in
D_{f}
; it is thus annihilated by
\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
. Further, there is a length two rightward semi-chain beginning at
u
which is not completed, in every term in the superposition. The databases are non-minimal, and the state is therefore annihilated by
\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}
. Further, the actual state
\ket{\psi}
is a linear combination of states of this form, so we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\left(\sum_{\pi\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}|\pi\rangle\langle\pi|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi}}}\otimes G^{(F)}_{\pi(x),{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{HKF}}}\right)\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=0,

(247)

and similarly for the sophisticated projector.
The claim now follows from triangle inequality.
∎

5.4
Preserved subspaces

We are now ready to show that the elegant subspace of sophisticated provides a faithful proxy of the query-valid subspace, proving
Lemma

5.4
. We restate it below.

See
5.4

Proof.

We can write

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\phi}-\ket{\mu}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}\ket{\mu}+\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}(\ket{\psi}-\ket{\mu})-\ket{\mu}}\right\|

(248)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}\ket{\mu}-\ket{\mu}}\right\|+\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}(\ket{\psi}-\ket{\mu})}\right\|

(249)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval},\perp}\ket{\mu}}\right\|+\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}(\ket{\psi}-\ket{\mu})}\right\|.

(250)

Therefore, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval},\perp}\ket{\mu}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right),

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}(\ket{\psi}-\ket{\mu})}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(251)

We perform the analysis for the case where the flip bit is
b=0
, the other case follows similarly.
Let us begin with the right hand equation. By definition, we have that
\ket{\psi}-\ket{\mu}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{fele},\perp}
is non-flip-elegant. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}(\ket{\psi}-\ket{\mu})}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}(\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}-\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}})}\right\|

(252)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\widetilde{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}(\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}-\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}})}\right\|+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(By
Corollary

5.9
)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(By
Lemma

5.15
)

In the other case, where
\ket{\mu}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval},\perp}\ket{\mu}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|

(253)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}}\right\|+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(By
Corollaries

5.9
and
5.13
)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(By
Remark

5.7
)

∎

Another important property which we can now prove is that the
\mathsf{cmfO}
operator approximately preserves the elegant subspace.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{fele}}
be a flip elegant quantum state supported on purifications of size at most
t
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{fele},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}}\right\|=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(254)

Proof.

Let us analyze the case for forward queries, the proof for inverse queries follows similarly.

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{ele},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{ele},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\mathsf{mfC}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}}\right\|

(255)

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{ele},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\overline{\mathsf{mfC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}^{\dagger}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}}\right\|+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right),

by
Corollary

5.13
, and the fact that
\mathsf{P}
preserves the subspace
\Pi^{\in\mathsf{db}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}
. Applying
Remark

5.7
, we find that the norm term is
0
, showing the claim.
∎

Using this lemma, we can show that the compressed masked Feistel oracle approximately preserves the sophisticated subspace.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
q
query quantum algorithm with intermediate operations
A_{0},\dots,A_{q}
, and denote the final state by

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{q}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(256)

Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\phi_{q}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{q^{4}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(257)

Proof.

We will prove this statement inductively. Clearly the
A_{t,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
operations cannot break validity nor increase the projector onto the non-sophisticated subspace, as operators on different registers commute. Therefore, we need only analyze the action of each query.

Suppose that the statement holds for the first
t
queries, and denote the state after these queries as

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}

\displaystyle=A_{t,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}.

(258)

We will split the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
register into a further
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}
component for the query registers.
We can write this state as

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}_{\ket{\mu}}+\underbrace{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}_{\ket{\delta}}+\underbrace{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}_{=0}.

(259)

We have
\left\|{\ket{\delta}}\right\|\leq\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}\right\|
. Turning to the first term, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\left(\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)\right)

(By
Lemma

5.4
)

(260)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(261)

Recalling from
Lemma

5.16
that
\mathsf{cmfO}
approximately preserves the flip elegant subspace, and that the flip elegant subspace is a subspace of sophisticed
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{ele}}\leq\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{soph}}
, we have
\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{ele},\perp}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}}\right\|=O(\sqrt{t^{2}/2^{n}})
. Plugging into
Equation

259
and applying triangle inequality, we find that

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}\right\|+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(262)

The claim now follows by induction.
∎

Finally, we can show that the
\mathcal{I}
operator acts as an approximate intertwiner between the two compressed permutation oracles, so long as the state is valid and close to sophisticated.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}
be a quantum state supported on purifications of size at most
t
, and which is valid. Let
\delta=\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}\right\|
be the size of the unsophisticated component.
Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|{(\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}-\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}})\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\delta+\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(263)

Proof.

We can write
\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}
as

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}_{\ket{\mu}}+\underbrace{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}_{\ket{\delta}}+\underbrace{\Pi^{\mathsf{qval},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}}_{=0}.

(264)

The final term being
0
follows from the fact that the state is valid.
Observe that
\left\|{\ket{\delta}}\right\|\leq\delta
, and the operator acting on the state is norm at most
2
, so this incurs an additive
O(\delta)
. Turning to the remaining component, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\ket{\mu}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYP}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(By
Lemma

5.4
)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{fele}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XP}}}\ket{\psi}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}}+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\right)

(By
Lemma

5.14
)

(265)

Plugging into
Equation

264
and applying triangle inequality shows the claim.
∎

We are now ready to show the main result of this manuscript, that the compressed permutation oracle construction is sound. We remind the reader that up to this point, we assumed only that the twirling distributions were cromulent. In the next section, we will make an explicit choice for these distributions.

5.5
Putting the pieces together

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
q
query quantum algorithm which queries a random permutation oracle. Suppose that the algorithm has a workspace register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
, and input/output registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}
of dimension
2^{2n+1}
and
2^{2n}
. The extra input bit indicates the direction of the query. We describe such an algorithm by a sequence of unitary operators
A_{0},\dots,A_{q}
acting on the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXY}}
system, interleaved with oracles
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}
which acts
\forall b\in\{0,1\},x,y\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\varphi,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}\ket{b,x}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}\ket{y}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}

\displaystyle=\ket{b,x}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}\ket{y\oplus\varphi^{1-2b}(x)}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}.

(266)

For simplicity, we will treat the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}
as subregisters of the adversary state
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
. We can then write the view of such an algorithm as the density matrix

\displaystyle\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\varphi\sim\mathbf{S}_{2^{2n}}}\left[\rho(A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathcal{O}_{\varphi,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}})\right].

(267)

Let us compare to the compressed oracle experiment, where we answer queries according to our compressed permutation oracle. In this experiment, we have a purifying register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}
in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{I})
, spanned by all injective partial functions from
\{0,1\}^{2n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{2n}
. In this case, we answer queries using the unitary
\mathsf{cpO}
, as described in
Section

3
. We think of the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}
register as being outside the algorithm’s view, so it is traced out. We can write the final state in this experiment as

\displaystyle\rho^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}\left[\rho(A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cpO}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}})\right].

(268)

Our main result is that these two views are indistinguishable unless the algorithm has made a number of queries comparable to the twelveth-root of the permutation size.

Theorem 5.19
.

Any
q
query quantum algorithm cannot distinguish the compressed permutation oracle from a uniform random permutation oracle on
[N]\simeq\{0,1\}^{2n}
except with advantage

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}-\rho^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{N^{1/4}}\right).

(269)

In particular, constant advantage requires
\Omega(\sqrt[12]{N})
queries.

Proof.

We will prove this statement by purifying. The natural purification of a random permutation oracle, which purifies
\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
, is to maintain a uniform superposition of permutation truth tables outside the view of the adversary. We will consider the alternate, more complicated purification described in
Section

4
, with a uniform twirl. This can be written as follows, where
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}
is the purifying register containing sub-registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega HKF}}
,

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathcal{O})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{mfP}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\otimes\sqrt{\frac{1}{(N!)^{2}(2^{n})^{3\cdot 2^{n}}}}\sum_{\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{S}_{N},h,k,f\in\mathbf{F}_{2^{n},2^{n}}}\ket{\pi,\omega,h,k,f}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}.

(270)

In other words, we take the twirling distribution
\mathcal{D}
to be uniform over
\mathbf{S}_{N}^{\times 2}
. This distribution is cromulent, as shown in
Section

4.4
. This oracle is perfectly indistinguishable from a random permutation oracle,
\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}[\rho(\ket{\psi^{(\mathcal{O})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}})]=\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
, as the permutation is a product of a random permutation (say
\pi
) with an independent permutation.

Now, by the standard compressed oracle theory, the state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\otimes\frac{1}{N!}\sum_{\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}\ket{\pi,\omega,\boldsymbol{\bot},\boldsymbol{\bot},\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}

(271)

is perfectly indistinguishable after tracing out the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}
register, in that we have

\displaystyle\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}[\rho(\ket{\psi^{(\mathcal{O})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}})]=\mathrm{Tr}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}[\rho(\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}})].

(272)

The purification of the compressed permutation oracle is

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}[\rho(\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}})]=\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}.

(273)

Now we can consider the hybrid states,

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{0}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathcal{I}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}

(274)

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{1}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathcal{I}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}

(275)

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{q}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}},

(276)

where
\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}=\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{0}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}=\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{q}}
. For
t\leq q
, let us define

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}=A_{t,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}},

(277)

observing that
\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}}\right\|=O(\sqrt{t^{4}/2^{n}})
by
Lemma

5.16
, and further the
\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{val}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
, lying entirely in the valid subspace as
\mathsf{cmfO}
preserves validity. Observing that
\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
clearly commutes with the
A_{t,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
operator, we can then compute the successive differences between hybrid states to be

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{t+1}}-\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{t}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{(\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}-\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}})\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{4}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(By
Lemma

5.14
)

(278)

Therefore, we have
\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}-\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\right\|=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{q^{6}}{2^{n}}}\right)
. This equation combined with
Lemma

2.2
proves the claim.
∎

6
Feistel security

Let
\mathsf{Feist}^{(m)}
denote the distribution induced on permutations by
m
rounds of Feistel with truly random round functions. This is query-indistinguishable from the case where the round functions are pseudorandom.
The size of all permutations considered is
N=4^{n}
. Our convention is that the first round is “left-to-right”, in that input
x
is transformed to
x_{L}\|(x_{R}\oplus h(x_{L}))
in the first round.

Our proof that the seven round Feistel construction is a qPRP is nearly identical to the soundness proof of our compressed permutation oracle. In particular, we will show that Feistel is indistinguishable from the compressed permutation oracle. To do so, we will apply the machinery we have developed for masked Feistel to the case where the twirling distribution
\mathcal{D}
draws
\pi
uniformly from
\mathsf{Feist}^{(2)}
, and
\omega
uniformly from
(\mathsf{Feist}^{(2)})^{-1}
. In other words, we define

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{D}}(\pi,\omega)

\displaystyle=p_{\mathsf{Feist}^{(2)}\times\mathsf{Feist}^{(2)}}(\pi,\omega^{-1}).

(279)

Note that this distribution is invariant under the transformation
(\pi,\omega)\mapsto(\omega^{-1},\pi^{-1})
, maintaining the flip symmetry of all our constructions thus far. The main technical component of this section is proving that this distribution is
cromulent
, which amounts to proving purely classical statistical properties. Once this is in place, we can use the results derived in the previous two sections to give a simple proof of security.

6.1
Twirling

We will study the statistical properties of the two round Feistel construction. In particular, we will show that
\mathcal{D}=\mathsf{Feist}^{(2)}\times(\mathsf{Feist}^{(2)})^{-1}
is a cromulent distribution. From the flip symmetry above, it will suffice to show the properties in
Definition

4.27
for the listed forward cases only. We can think of sampling from
\mathcal{D}_{I}
as a procedure in which we assign inputs to the constituent functions in stages. We use
D^{(\pi)}_{1}
and
D^{(\pi)}_{2}
to denote the partial databases representing the two Feistel functions composing
\pi
throughout this procedure. We use
D^{(\omega)}_{1}
to represent the last Feistel round, and
D^{(\omega)}_{2}
to represent the second to last, in
\omega
. Observe that
D^{(\cdot)}_{1}
is left-to-right, and
D^{(\cdot)}_{2}
is right-to-left, for both
\pi
and
\omega
. We stress that this is purely a classical procedure, which is useful as a tool to prove probabilistic properties about this distribution. We use ideas from
Definition

4.4
in this description.

1.

For each
x\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)
, sample random values for
D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L})
and
D^{(\pi)}_{2}(x_{R}\oplus D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L}))
, and for each
y\in\mathsf{Im}(I)
, sample random values for
D^{(\omega)}_{1}(y_{L})
and
D^{(\omega)}_{2}(y_{R}\oplus D^{(\omega)}_{1}(y_{L}))
.

2.

Observing that
A=\pi*I*\omega
is now fully defined, if
A
is non-allowable, restart the procedure, and otherwise continue.

3.

Sample the remaining undefined points at uniform random.

Now we observe a number of symmetries in this distribution. First, let us define a
right shift
in
\pi
, parameterized by an
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, as the transformation
(D^{(\pi)}_{1},D^{(\pi)}_{2})\mapsto_{s}(D^{(\pi)^{\prime}}_{1},D^{(\pi)^{\prime}}_{2})
where

\displaystyle D^{(\pi)}_{1}(w_{1})=z_{1}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow D^{(\pi)^{\prime}}_{1}(w_{1})=z_{1}\oplus s,

(280)

\displaystyle D^{(\pi)}_{2}(z_{2})=w_{2}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow D^{(\pi)^{\prime}}_{2}(z_{2}\oplus s)=w_{2}.

(281)

Similarly, a right shift in
\omega
parameterized by an
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is the transformation
(D^{(\omega)}_{1},D^{(\omega)}_{2})\mapsto_{s}(D^{(\omega)^{\prime}}_{1},D^{(\omega)^{\prime}}_{2})
where

\displaystyle D^{(\omega)}_{1}(w_{1})=z_{1}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow D^{(\omega)^{\prime}}_{1}(w_{1})=z_{1}\oplus s,

(282)

\displaystyle D^{(\omega)}_{2}(z_{2})=w_{2}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow D^{(\omega)^{\prime}}_{2}(z_{2}\oplus s)=w_{2}.

(283)

We define a left shift as a joint transformation on
(\pi,\omega)
, again parameterized by an
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, which maps
(D^{(\pi)}_{2},D^{(\omega)}_{2})\mapsto_{s}(D^{(\pi)^{\prime}}_{2},D^{(\omega)^{\prime}}_{2})
where

\displaystyle D^{(\pi)}_{2}(z_{\pi})=w_{\pi}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow D^{(\pi)^{\prime}}_{2}(z_{\pi})=w_{\pi}\oplus s,

(284)

\displaystyle D^{(\omega)}_{2}(z_{\omega})=w_{\omega}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow D^{(\omega)^{\prime}}_{2}(z_{\omega})=w_{\omega}\oplus s.

(285)

We can equivalently think of sampling from
\mathcal{D}_{I}
with additional re-randomization, as illustrated by these symmetries. In the following lemma, inside of each step the order does not matter (e.g. the selections in step (1) can be made in any order, as long as they all occur before the selections in step (4)).

Lemma 6.1
.

The following procedure is equivalent to sampling
(\pi,\omega)
from
\mathcal{D}_{I}
, where
\pi
is defined by the two round Feistel
D^{(\pi)}_{1},D^{(\pi)}_{2}
and
\omega
is defined by the inverse two round Feistel of
D^{(\omega)}_{1},D^{(\omega)}_{2}
.

1.

For each
x\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)
, sample random values for
D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L})
and
D^{(\pi)}_{2}(x_{R}\oplus D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L}))
, and for each
y\in\mathsf{Im}(I)
, sample random values for
D^{(\omega)}_{1}(y_{L})
and
D^{(\omega)}_{2}(y_{R}\oplus D^{(\omega)}_{1}(y_{L}))
. Noting that this suffices to define
A=\pi*I*\omega
, do this conditioned on the fact that
A
is allowable.

2.

Sample random
s\sim\{0,1\}^{n}
, perform a right shift by
s
on
\pi
.

3.

Sample random
s^{\prime}\sim\{0,1\}^{n}
, perform a right shift by
s^{\prime}
on
\omega
.

4.

Sample the remaining undefined points of the
D_{i}^{(\pi)}
and
D_{i}^{(\omega)}
at uniform random.

5.

Sample random
s^{\prime\prime}\sim\{0,1\}^{n}
, perform a left shift by
s^{\prime\prime}
on
(\pi,\omega)
.

Proof.

Recall that allowability of
A=\pi*I*\omega
is a condition on only the left substrings of
A
. A random right shift does not affect the left substrings of
A
, and so the right shifts in steps (2) and (3) do not change the distribution. Furthermore, the allowability conditions are a set of linear equations over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{n}
on the left substrings with
2
or
4
variables in each. Shifting all of the variables of such an equation does not change its truth/falsehood, as every shift
s
has order
2
with
s\oplus s=0
, so the last step does not affect allowability.
∎

Note that we can reason similarly about, e.g.,
\mathcal{D}_{x,I}
, simply adding the condition of allowing
x
in step (1). The same argument implies that the random shifts do not change the truth or falsehood of this condition.
With this in place, we are ready to prove that
\mathcal{D}
satisfies the cromulent conditions.

Lemma 6.2
.

Fix some
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
,
x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{I}}[(\pi,\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{x,I}]

\displaystyle=1-O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right)

(286)

when
t=O(2^{n/3})
.

Note the additional condition on
t
compared to the uniform twirl case; this nonetheless holds in our application.

Proof.

We will use the characterization in
Lemma

6.1
. There are two cases to consider.

1.

There is an
x^{\prime}\in I
with
x_{L}=x_{L}^{\prime}
. Let
X\subset I
be the set of such
x^{\prime}
’s. Let us imagine selecting the value
D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L})
, and the corresponding values in
D^{(\pi)}_{2}
, last in step (1). Prior to this selection, define
Z=\{x^{\prime\prime}_{R}\oplus D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x^{\prime\prime}_{L})\,:\,x^{\prime\prime}\in I,x^{\prime\prime}_{L}\neq x_{L}\}
as the set of values on the right wire which have been determined, where
|Z|<t
. We use
Z^{\prime}=\{z\,:\,\exists x^{\prime}\in X\cup\{x\}\text{ s.t. }x^{\prime}_{R}\oplus z\in Z\}
as the set of assignments to
D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L})
which would cause a collision with these values on the right wire. The probability of selecting some
D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L})
which is not in
Z^{\prime}
is uniform, so it only remains to analyze the probability of some selection in
Z^{\prime}
. Such a selection may be more likely if it causes some
x^{\prime}
to immediately satisfy allowability. In such a case, there are a factor
2^{n}/(2^{n}-O(t^{2}))=1+O(t^{2}/2^{n})
more ways to choose the database values, as there would have been
2^{n}-O(t^{2})
allowable values of
D^{(\pi)}_{2}(x^{\prime}_{R}\oplus D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x^{\prime}_{L}))
otherwise by
Lemma

4.10
. Over the at most
t
values in
X
, this gives a factor at most
(1+O(t^{2}/2^{n}))^{t}=1+O(t^{3}/2^{n})=O(1)
, meaning values in
Z^{\prime}
are at most a constant factor more likely than values outside. It follows that values in
Z^{\prime}
have probability
O(2^{-n})
. We further have
|Z^{\prime}|=O(t^{2})
by a simple counting argument.

Over the random choice (conditioned on prior events) of
D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L})
, we then have

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{I}}[D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L})\in Z^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=O(t^{2}/2^{n})

(287)

from the union bound. Supposing that this event does not happen, the value
D^{(\pi)}_{2}(x_{R}\oplus D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L}))
will be selected at uniform random in step (4). Once again by
Lemma

4.10
, this will lead to an allowable value of
\pi(x)_{L}
with probability
1-O(t^{2}/2^{n})
, showing the claim.

2.

There is no
x^{\prime}\in I
with
x_{L}=x_{L}^{\prime}
. Then, the value
D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L})
will be selected in step (4), and will result in a value
D^{(\pi)}_{2}(x_{R}\oplus D^{(\pi)}_{1}(x_{L}))
which is not already selected with probability
1-O(t/2^{n})
. Similar to the previous case, conditioned on this value being sampled fresh in step (4) there is once again a probability
1-O(t^{2}/2^{n})
of
x
being disallowed, proving the claim.

∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Fix some
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
,
y\neq y^{\prime}\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)
,
x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)
and
l\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}

(288)

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}=l]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2n}}\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{3n}}\right).

(289)

Proof.

We will use the characterization in
Lemma

6.1
. For the first equation, consider performing steps (1-4). Then, step (5) will re-randomize the value of
\omega^{-1}(y)
, such that it will satisfy
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=l
with probability
2^{-n}
.

For the second equation, let us begin by analyzing the probability that
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}
after steps (1-4). There are the following cases.

1.

y_{L}=y_{L}^{\prime}
. Then, after the selection of
D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L})
, it is guaranteed that
y_{R}\oplus D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L})\neq y_{R}^{\prime}\oplus D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L}^{\prime})
. Let us define

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=y_{R}\oplus D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L}),

\displaystyle z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=y_{R}\oplus D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L}).

(290)

Now, consider performing all of the selections which can be performed prior to selecting
D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z)
. WLOG we can assume that
D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z^{\prime})
is already selected (otherwise, swap
y
and
y^{\prime}
). The selection of
D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z)
will occur either at the end of step (1) (in which case there are at least
2^{n}-O(t^{2})
admissible values) or at the end of step (4) (in which case there are
2^{n}
admissible values), depending on the prior selections. In all cases, we have shown

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{n}}\leq\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}\,|\,y_{L}=y_{L}^{\prime}]\leq\frac{1}{2^{n}-O(t^{2})}.

(291)

2.

y_{L}\neq y_{L}^{\prime}
, and there is
y^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{Im}(I)
with
y^{\prime\prime}_{L}=y_{L}
and
y^{\prime\prime\prime}_{L}=y^{\prime}_{L}
. We will call this case
E_{2}
. Consider in step (1) first selecting all of the values relevant to
\pi
, all of the relevant values for
D_{1}^{(\omega)}
except
D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L})
, and none of the values for
D_{2}^{(\omega)}
. Let us define
z^{\prime}=y_{R}^{\prime}\oplus D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L}^{\prime})
. In the selection of
D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L})
, there are at least
2^{n}-O(t^{2})
admissible values, i.e. values not immediately violating the allowability condition. If we define
z=y_{R}\oplus D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L})
, then we will have
z=z^{\prime}
with probability no more than
\frac{1}{2^{n}-O(t^{2})}
; call this event
E_{\mathsf{col}}
.

In the case of
E_{\mathsf{col}}
, we are guaranteed that
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}\neq\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}
, as if there is a right collision there cannot be a left collision. In the other case, we can consider once again selecting
D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z)
after
D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z^{\prime})
, and after all the (other) selections in step (1) are made. Similar to the previous case, depending on the step in which this occurs, the probability of
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}
lies between
\frac{1}{2^{n}}
and
\frac{1}{2^{n}-O(t^{2})}
. We then have

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}\,|\,E_{2}]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}\,|\,E_{2},E_{\mathsf{col}}]+

\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}\,|\,E_{2},\neg E_{\mathsf{col}}]}_{=0}

(292)

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{n}-O(t^{2})}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{n}\pm O(t^{2})}\right)

(293)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{2n}}\right)

(294)

3.

There is no
y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{Im}(I)
with
y^{\prime\prime}_{L}=y_{L}
, or no such
y^{\prime\prime}
with
y^{\prime\prime}_{L}=y^{\prime}_{L}
. We can WLOG assume the first one. We call this case
E_{3}
. Now, we imagine performing all of the selections in step (1), as well as selecting
D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L}^{\prime})
to define
z^{\prime}=y_{R}^{\prime}\oplus D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L}^{\prime})
, and selecting
D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z^{\prime})
to define
w^{\prime}=y_{L}^{\prime}\oplus D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z^{\prime})
(this selection may occur in step (1), or as the first selection in step (4)). Now let
d=y_{L}\oplus w^{\prime}
. Let
E_{d}
denote the event that
d
appears in the image of
D_{2}^{(\omega)}
at this stage. By the shift invariance of allowability, and the fact that
D_{2}^{(\omega)}
is of size at most
t+1
, we have that
E_{d}
occurs with probability
O(t/2^{n})
.

Now we consider selecting
D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L})
and defining
z=y_{R}\oplus D_{1}^{(\omega)}(y_{L})
. Let
E_{z}
denote the event that
z
is in the domain of
D_{2}^{(\omega)}
. There are at most
t+1
such values, so
E_{z}
occurs with probability
O(t/2^{n})
over the randomness of the selection. Note that
E_{z}
is independent of
E_{d}
. If
E_{z}
occurs, then the value
w=y_{L}\oplus D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z)
is already defined, and we have that
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}
can only occur if
D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z)=d
, i.e. if
E_{d}
occurred.

Turning to the other case, we consider randomly selecting
D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z)
, which defines
w=y_{L}\oplus D_{2}^{(\omega)}(z)
. With probability
2^{-n}
, we will have
w=w^{\prime}
. This occurs regardless of
E_{d}
. Assembling all the cases, we can write

\displaystyle\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}\,|\,E_{3}]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}\,|\,E_{3},E_{d},E_{z}]}_{O(t^{2}/2^{2n})}+

\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}\,|\,E_{3},\neg E_{d},E_{z}]}_{=0}+

\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{\Pr_{(\pi,\omega)\sim\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}[\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}\,|\,E_{3},\neg E_{z}]}_{(1-O(t/2^{n}))(2^{-n})}

(295)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{2n}}\right).

(296)

Together, these cases imply that we have
\omega^{-1}(y)_{L}=\omega^{-1}(y^{\prime})_{L}
with probability
2^{-n}\pm O(t^{2}/2^{2n})
, prior to step (5). Over the random shift in step (5), we will have
\omega^{-1}(y)=l
with probability
2^{-n}
, independent of the prior event. Multiplying together gives the claim.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

For any
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
,
x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)
,
y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)
, and
\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{R}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}
with
A=\pi*I*\omega
, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}}(\pi,\omega)}{(N-t)}}-\sqrt{\frac{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}{|\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),A}|}}

\displaystyle=\pm O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}}(\pi,\omega)}{N-t}}\right).

(297)

Proof.

Observe that we have

\displaystyle\frac{p_{\mathcal{D}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}}(\pi,\omega)}{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}

\displaystyle=1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{N-t}{\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),\mathbf{A}}}

\displaystyle=1\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}}\right),

(298)

the first by an argument analogous to
Lemma

6.2
and the second by
Lemma

4.10
. If we let
a=(N-t)/p_{\mathcal{D}_{I[x\rightarrow y]}}(\pi,\omega)
, then we can write

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{p_{\mathcal{D}_{x,I}}(\pi,\omega)}{|\mathbf{V}_{\pi(x),A}|}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a(1\pm O(t^{2}/2^{n})}}

(299)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\pm O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{n}\cdot\sqrt{a}}\right),

(300)

where the last line follows from the taylor expansion of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{a+x}}
about
x=0
.
∎

6.2
Putting the pieces together

As in
Section

5
, let
\mathcal{A}
be a
q
query quantum algorithm which queries a permutation oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
for
\varphi\sim\mathsf{Feist}^{(7)}
. Suppose that the algorithm has a workspace register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
, and input/output registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}
of dimension
2^{2n+1}
and
2^{2n}
. The extra input bit indicates the direction of the query. We describe such an algorithm by a sequence of unitary operators
A_{0},\dots,A_{q}
acting on the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXY}}
system, interleaved with oracles
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}
which acts
\forall b\in\{0,1\},x,y\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\varphi,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}}\ket{b,x}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}\ket{y}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}

\displaystyle=\ket{b,x}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}\ket{y\oplus\varphi^{1-2b}(x)}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{Y}}.

(301)

For simplicity, we will treat the registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}
as subregisters of the adversary state
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
. We can then write the view of such an algorithm on querying feistel as the density matrix

\displaystyle\rho^{(\mathsf{Feist})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\varphi\sim\mathsf{Feist}^{(7)}}\left[\rho(A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathcal{O}_{\varphi,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}})\right].

(302)

We can write the view of the same algorithm querying a truly random permutation as

\displaystyle\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\varphi\sim\mathbf{S}_{N}}\left[\rho(A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathcal{O}_{\varphi,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}})\right].

(303)

We will show that these two views are indistinguishable unless the algorithm has made a number of queries comparable to the twelveth-root of the permutation size.

Theorem 6.5
.

Any
q
query quantum algorithm cannot the seven round balanced Feistel construction from a uniform random permutation oracle on
[N]
except with advantage

\displaystyle\left\|{\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}-\rho^{(\mathsf{Feist})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{N^{1/4}}\right).

(304)

In particular, constant advantage requires
\Omega(\sqrt[12]{N})
bidirectional queries.

Proof.

We will prove this statement by purifying. We will use our compressed oracle as an intermediary between the two, where we have a purifying register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}
. We can write the final state in this experiment as

\displaystyle\rho^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}\left[\rho(A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cpO}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}})\right].

(305)

By
Theorem

5.19
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\rho^{(\mathsf{cpO})}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}-\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{N^{1/4}}\right).

(306)

We will therefore analyze the compresed permutation oracle experiment in place of the ideal permutation oracle.

To purify the seven round Feistel construction, we will follow the idea described in
Section

4
. In particular, we will consider the two leftmost Feistel rounds to define the permutation
\pi
, the two rightmost Feistel rounds to define the permutation
\omega
, and the internal three rounds to correspond to the
h,k,f
functions. This can be written as follows, where
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}
is the purifying register containing sub-registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{\Pi\Omega HKF}}
,

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathcal{O})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{mfP}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\otimes\sqrt{\frac{1}{(2^{n})^{3\cdot 2^{n}}}}\sum_{\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{S}_{N},h,k,f\in\mathbf{F}_{2^{n},2^{n}}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega,h,k,f}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}.

(307)

where
\mathcal{D}
is defined as the distribution
\mathsf{Feist}^{(2)}\times(\mathsf{Feist}^{(2)})^{-1}
. This distribution is cromulent, as shown in
Section

6.1
, meaning we can apply the results of
Section

5
. This oracle is perfectly indistinguishable from the seven round Feistel construction,
\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}[\rho(\ket{\psi^{(\mathcal{O})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AXYP}})]=\rho^{(\mathsf{Feist})}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
.
Now, by the standard compressed oracle theory, the state

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{mfC})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\otimes\sum_{\pi,\omega\in\mathbf{S}_{N}}\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{D}}(\pi,\omega)}\ket{\pi,\omega,\boldsymbol{\bot},\boldsymbol{\bot},\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}

(308)

is perfectly indistinguishable after tracing out the
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}
register, in that we have

\displaystyle\rho^{(\mathcal{O})}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}[|\psi^{(\mathcal{O})}\rangle\langle\psi^{(\mathcal{O})}|]

(309)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}[|\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}\rangle\langle\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}|].

(310)

For comparison, we will take the following purification of the compressed oracle,

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}[\rho(\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}})]=\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}.

(311)

Now we can consider the hybrid states,

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{0}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathcal{I}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}

(312)

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{1}}

\displaystyle=A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathcal{I}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}

(313)

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{q}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}},

(314)

where
\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}=\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{0}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}=\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{q}}
. For
t\leq q
, let us define

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}=A_{t,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\dots\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}\ket{{\mathbf{P}(\boldsymbol{\bot})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}},

(315)

observing that
\left\|{\Pi^{\mathsf{soph},\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}}\right\|=O(\sqrt{t^{4}/2^{n}})
by
Lemma

5.16
, and further the
\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}\in\Pi^{\mathsf{val}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
, lying entirely in the valid subspace as
\mathsf{cmfO}
preserves validity. Observing that
\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}
clearly commutes with the
A_{t,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
operator, we can then compute the successive differences between hybrid states to be

\displaystyle\left\|{\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{t+1}}-\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{Hyb})}_{t}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{(\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\cdot\mathsf{cmfO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}-\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}})\ket{\phi_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}}}\right\|

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{4}}{2^{n}}}\right).

(By
Lemma

5.14
)

(316)

Therefore, we have
\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{P}}}\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cmfO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AP}}-\ket{\psi^{(\mathsf{cpO})}}_{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\right\|=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{q^{6}}{2^{n}}}\right)
. This equation combined with
Lemma

2.2
proves the claim.
∎

7
Search bounds

We can also apply this framework to prove new search type query lower bounds. The most important ingredient in such bounds is the fundamental lemma, which relates the compressed permutation oracle database to points the algorithm “knows”. This result can be applied to show lower bounds for various search problems in which the goal is to find a set of input output points having some property.
All of our bounds follow the same rough format.

1.

Switch to the compressed permutation oracle.

2.

Show that the database is unlikely to contain a solution after
q
queries.

3.

Apply
Lemma

3.3
to conclude that the adversary is unlikely to have found a solution.

The first step is straightforward, while the second step varies in complexity depending on the problem. Often, it is enough to reason combinatorially about the databases, analogous to compressed function oracles. We will show how to use the fundamental lemma to apply the final step in general.

Throughout this section, our bounds will incur a loss due to the distinguishing advantage against the compressed permutation oracle. We quantify this by the loss term

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{N^{1/4}}\right),

(317)

and this term is often the loosest. Improving the soundness error of our construction would improve all the lower bounds derived. Most would be tight, ignoring the
{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}
term.

To formalize this procedure, we will capture search problems by a target predicate
\mathcal{R}\subset([N]^{\times 2})^{*}
, or a set of finite lists of input-output pairs representing the set of potential solutions. Then, we consider an adversary
\mathcal{A}
making
q
quantum queries to a permutation oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
. The predicate
\mathcal{R}
is known to the adversary (meaning its intermediate operations may depend on
\mathcal{R}
), although naturally the underlying permutation is not, outside of the information available by querying the oracle. The goal of the adversary is to find some input-output pairs of the permutation which satisfy the predicate. In particular, the game proceeds as follows.

Game 1
.

Predicate search.

1.

A random
\varphi\sim\mathbf{S}_{N}
is selected to construct
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
.

2.

An adversary
\mathcal{A}
makes
q
quantum queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
, and outputs a list
\mathbf{x}=[(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})]
.

3.

The adversary wins if
\varphi(x_{i})=y_{i}
for all
i
, and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{R}
.

Alternatively, we could consider a game in which the adversary queries the compressed permutation oracle, and the final condition involves only analyzing the compressed oracle database. We say an
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
satisfies
\mathcal{R}
, denoted
I\cap\mathcal{R}\neq\emptyset
, if there exists
\mathbf{x}=[(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})]
such that
I(x_{i})=y_{i}
for all
i\in[l]
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{R}
. We define the predicate projector

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{R}}\ket{I}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\ket{I}&\text{(If $I\cap\mathcal{R}\neq\emptyset$)}\\
0&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(318)

which projects onto databases which contain a set of input-output pairs in the predicate.

Game 2
.

Predicate search with compressed oracles.

1.

The compressed databases is initialized to
\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}
.

2.

The adversary
\mathcal{A}
with workspace
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}
makes
q
quantum queries to
\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}
.

3.

The projective measurement
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{R}}=\{\Pi_{\mathcal{R}},\Pi_{\mathcal{R}}^{\perp}\}
is applied to register
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}
. The adversary wins if the first outcome is achieved.

It turns out that the winning probability of the second game provides an upper bound on the first. We can compare the same adversary across the two games by simply ignoring the output of
\mathcal{A}
in the second game. This is analogous to the result of
[
Zha19
]
relating the compressed function oracle database to the winning probability of a search game.

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
wins
2
with probability
p_{2}
, and wins
1
with probability
p_{1}
. Then we have the bound

\displaystyle\sqrt{p_{1}}\leq\sqrt{p_{2}}+\frac{l}{\sqrt{N-q-l}}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q+l)}.

(319)

This bound also implies

\displaystyle p_{1}=O\left(p_{2}+\frac{l^{2}}{N-q-l}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q+l)}^{2}\right).

(320)

Proof.

For
1
, we can suppose WLOG that the adversary outputs some
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{R}
. If not, then modify the last step to check whether the output satisfies the predicate, and if needed switch to some output which satisfies the predicate. This cannot decrease the success probability, as the adversary is guaranteed to lose if
\mathbf{x}\not\in\mathcal{R}
. Call this modified adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Now, consider running
1
with the compressed permutation oracle and adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, replacing the final check with the projector
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}^{(\mathcal{O}_{\varphi})}
from
Lemma

3.3
. The resultant state is a distance at most
{\mathsf{Dist}(q+l)}
, by the definition of the distinguishing advantage.

Then, we can replace this projector with
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}^{(\mathsf{cpO})}
by
Lemma

3.3
, and the resultant state is a distance at most
\frac{l}{\sqrt{N-q-l}}
. Now, this projector can only succeed if there is a tuple of input-output pairs in the database which are also in
\mathcal{R}
, i.e. the state is in
\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}
. The magnitude of this component is then at most
\sqrt{p_{2}}
.
∎

7.1
Simple examples

The simplest example of a problem for which we can show a lower bound is the one-more problem. In this problem, an adversary makes
q
queries to a random permutation and is tasked with finding
q+1
input-output pairs.

{problem}

Sample
\varphi\sim\mathbf{S}_{N}
, to produce two way accessible oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
.
\mathcal{A}
makes
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
, and succeeds if it finds
\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{q+1},y_{q+1})\}
such that
\varphi(x_{i})=y_{i}
for all
i\in[q+1]
, and
x_{i}\neq x_{i^{\prime}}
for
i\neq i^{\prime}
.

This problem does not appear to be in the scope of existing techniques for permutation problems. However, it is a simple corollary of our framework.

Theorem 7.2
.

No
q
query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
can solve the one more problem,
Section

7.1
, with probability exceeding

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{2}}{N}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}\right).

(321)

In particular, exponential queries are required for constant error.

Proof.

We can analyze this game in the compressed permutation oracle picture, as in
2
. In this context, the predicate
\mathcal{R}
is the set of all length
q+1
lists of distinct input-output pairs. After
q
queries, the database is of size at most
q
by
Lemma

3.2
. Therefore, no satisfying list can appear in the database, so the success probability of the adversary in this game is zero. The claim now follows from
Lemma

7.1
.
∎

Another simple problem outside the scope of existing techniques is the cycle finding problem. In this problem, the adversary is tasked with finding a cycle in the permutation.

{problem}

Sample
\varphi\sim\mathbf{S}_{N}
, to produce two way accessible oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
.
\mathcal{A}
makes
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
, and succeeds if it finds
x_{1},\dots,x_{l}
such that
\varphi(x_{i})=x_{i+1\mod l}
for all
i\in[l]
.

Theorem 7.3
.

No
q
query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
can solve cycle finding,
Section

7.1
, with probability exceeding

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{N}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}\right).

(322)

In particular, exponential queries are required for constant error.

Proof.

We use the framework of
2
, where the predicate
\mathcal{R}
is the set of all cycles. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algorithm with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
containing query subregisters
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}
, and intermediate operations
A_{0},\dots,A_{q}
on this space. The success probability of this algorithm is given by

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}=\left\|{\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|^{2}.

(323)

To bound this quantity, we will show a bound on the commutator norm

\displaystyle\left\|{[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}]}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}\right).

(324)

To do this, we can modify the compression operator such that it cannot add cycles to the database. In particular, fix an
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
and
x\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)
. We will first define an operator on
\mathcal{H}(I|^{x})
, writing

\displaystyle\ket{\widetilde{+}_{x,I}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\mathsf{Dom}(I)\cup\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}\ket{I[x\rightarrow y]}

(325)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{x,I}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{1}-|I\rangle\langle I|-|\widetilde{+}_{x,I}\rangle\langle\widetilde{+}_{x,I}|+|\widetilde{+}_{x,I}\rangle\langle I|+|I\rangle\langle\widetilde{+}_{x,I}|

(326)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in[N],I\in\mathbf{I}^{x}}|x\rangle\langle x|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathsf{pC}_{x,I,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}.

(327)

Note that the above are unitary operators so long as the database is of size less than
N/2
, which is anyways beyond where the bound trivializes.
Observe that
\left\|{\ket{\widetilde{+}_{x,I}}-\ket{+_{x,I}}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O(\sqrt{t/N})
, so
\left\|{\mathsf{pC}-\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O(\sqrt{t/N})
by
Lemma

2.3
. Now we have

\displaystyle[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=0,

(328)
