\displaystyle[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=0,

(329)

as
\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}
cannot add a cycle to the database.
If we write

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{cpO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}

\displaystyle=|0\rangle\langle 0|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\dagger})+|1\rangle\langle 1|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger})

(330)

\displaystyle=|0\rangle\langle 0|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\dagger})+|1\rangle\langle 1|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}\cdot(\mathsf{F}^{\dagger}\mathsf{F})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot(\mathsf{F}^{\dagger}\mathsf{F})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}),

(331)

then we have
\left\|{\mathsf{cpO}-\widetilde{\mathsf{cpO}}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O(\sqrt{t/N})
, and
\widetilde{\mathsf{cpO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}=\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\widetilde{\mathsf{cpO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}
, so the claimed
Equation

324
follows. Now we can write

\displaystyle\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|

(332)

\displaystyle=\left\|{A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}A_{q-1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{q}{N}}\right)

(333)

\displaystyle\dots

(334)

\displaystyle=\left\|{A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|+\sum_{t=1}^{q}O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t}{N}}\right)

(335)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{q^{3}}{N}}\right).

(336)

∎

7.2
A general search lower bound

The proof strategy developed for the cycle finding lower bounding can be applied much more broadly, to give a lower bound for any search problem. This bound is in terms of a quantity we refer to as the
sparsity
of the underlying predicate. Intuitively, this quantity measures the likelihood that each new query results in a solution being added to the database. Recalling that we allow forwards and inverse queries, we define sparsity in a two-sided way that allows both direction of query.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\subset([N]^{\times 2})^{*}
be a predicate, and
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
such that
I\cap\mathcal{R}=\emptyset
. Then we define the
I
-sparsity
s_{I}
and
t
-sparsity
s_{t}
as

\displaystyle s_{I}(\mathcal{R})

\displaystyle=\max\Bigg\{\max_{x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Dom}(I)}|\{y\not\in\mathsf{Im}(I)\,:\,I[x\rightarrow y]\cap\mathcal{R}\neq\emptyset\}|,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\max_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)}|\{x\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)\,:\,I[x\rightarrow y]\cap\mathcal{R}\neq\emptyset\}|\Bigg\}

\displaystyle s_{t}(\mathcal{R})

\displaystyle=\max_{I\in\mathbf{I}_{\leq t}\,:\,I\cap\mathcal{R}=\emptyset}s_{I}(\mathcal{R}).

(337)

Now, we can give a lower bound for any predicate search problem in terms of this quantity. Note that special cases of this theorem are known for predicates of constant size, meaning subsets of
([N]^{\times 2})^{O(1)}
, due to
[
MMW24
,
Coj+25
]
.

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\subset([N]^{\times 2})^{*}
be a predicate, and
\mathcal{A}
be a quantum algorithm making
q
bi-directional queries to a random permutation of size
[N]
to output a list
\mathbf{x}=[(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})]
of input-output pairs. Then,
\mathcal{A}
wins
1
under predicate
\mathcal{R}
with probability at most

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=O\left({\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}+\frac{l^{2}}{N}+\left(\sum_{t=0}^{q-1}\sqrt{\frac{s_{t}(\mathcal{R})}{N}}\right)^{2}\right).

(338)

Proof.

We use the framework of
2
. Let
\mathcal{A}
act on Hillbert space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}
containing query registers
{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XY}}
with intermediate operations
A_{0},\dots,A_{q}
on this space. The success probability of this algorithm is given by

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}=\left\|{\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|^{2}.

(339)

To bound this quantity, we will show a bound on the commutator norm

\displaystyle\left\|{[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}]}\right\|_{\leq t}

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{s_{t}(\mathcal{R})}{N}}\right).

(340)

To do this, we can modify the compression operator such that it preserves dissatisfaction of
\mathcal{R}
perfectly. In particular, fix an
I\in\mathbf{I}_{t}
and
x\not\in\mathsf{Dom}(I)
. We define the set
\mathcal{R}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}
to be the set of images whose (forwards) assignment on
x
would result in the predicate being satisfied, or the empty set if the predicate is already satisfied. Formally,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{y\,:\,I[x\rightarrow y]\in\mathcal{R}\}&\text{(If $I\cap\mathcal{R}=\emptyset$)}\\
\emptyset&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(341)

Observe that
|\mathcal{R}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}|\leq s_{I}(\mathcal{R})
. Then we write

\displaystyle\ket{\widetilde{+}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\mathcal{R}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}\cup\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\sum_{y\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)\setminus\mathcal{R}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}}\ket{I[x\rightarrow y]}

(342)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{1}-|I\rangle\langle I|-|\widetilde{+}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}\rangle\langle\widetilde{+}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}|+|\widetilde{+}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}\rangle\langle I|+|I\rangle\langle\widetilde{+}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}|

(343)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\rightarrow}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in[N],I\in\mathbf{I}^{x}}|x\rangle\langle x|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}.

(344)

In the other direction, we define the set
\mathcal{R}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}
to be the set of preimages whose (inverse) assignment on
y
would result in the predicate being satisfied, and otherwise the empty set. Formally,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\{x\,:\,(I[y\rightarrow x])^{-1}\in\mathcal{R}\}&\text{(If $I^{-1}\cap\mathcal{R}=\emptyset$)}\\
\emptyset&\text{(Otherwise)}\end{cases}

(345)

Observe that
|\mathcal{R}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}|\leq s_{I^{-1}}(\mathcal{R})
. Then we write

\displaystyle\ket{\widetilde{+}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\mathcal{R}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}\cup\mathsf{Im}(I)|}}\sum_{x\in[N]\setminus\mathsf{Im}(I)\setminus\mathcal{R}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}}\ket{I[y\rightarrow x]}

(346)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{1}-|I\rangle\langle I|-|\widetilde{+}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}\rangle\langle\widetilde{+}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}|+|\widetilde{+}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}\rangle\langle I|+|I\rangle\langle\widetilde{+}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}|

(347)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\leftarrow}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{y\in[N],I\in\mathbf{I}^{y}}|y\rangle\langle y|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}.

(348)

Observe that
\left\|{\ket{\widetilde{+}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}}-\ket{+_{x,I}}}\right\|=O(\sqrt{|\mathcal{R}_{x,I}^{\rightarrow}|/N})
, and
\left\|{\ket{\widetilde{+}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}}-\ket{+_{y,I}}}\right\|=O(\sqrt{|\mathcal{R}_{y,I}^{\leftarrow}|/N})
. It follows that
\left\|{\mathsf{pC}-\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}^{\rightarrow}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O(\sqrt{s_{t}(\mathcal{R})/N})
and
\left\|{\mathsf{pC}-\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}^{\leftarrow}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O(\sqrt{s_{t}(\mathcal{R})/N})
by
Lemma

2.3
and the prior observation.
Now we have

\displaystyle[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\rightarrow}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\leftarrow}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=0,

(349)

\displaystyle[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}},\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=0,

(350)

as the modified operators cannot add any input-output pair which causes
\mathcal{R}
to be satisfied.
If we write

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{cpO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}YI}

\displaystyle=|0\rangle\langle 0|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\rightarrow}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\rightarrow,\dagger})+|1\rangle\langle 1|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\leftarrow}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\leftarrow,\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger})

(351)

\displaystyle=|0\rangle\langle 0|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\rightarrow}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\rightarrow,\dagger})+|1\rangle\langle 1|_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes(\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\leftarrow}\cdot(\mathsf{F}^{\dagger}\mathsf{F})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\cdot(\mathsf{F}^{\dagger}\mathsf{F})_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{pC}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XI}}}^{\leftarrow,\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{F}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}^{\dagger}),

then we have
\left\|{\mathsf{cpO}-\widetilde{\mathsf{cpO}}}\right\|_{\leq t}=O(\sqrt{s_{t}(\mathcal{R})/N})
, and
\widetilde{\mathsf{cpO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}=\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\widetilde{\mathsf{cpO}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{XYI}}}
, so the claimed
Equation

340
follows. Now we can write

\displaystyle\sqrt{p_{\mathcal{A}}}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|

(352)

\displaystyle=\left\|{A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}A_{q-1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{s_{q-1}(\mathcal{R})}{N}}\right)

(353)

\displaystyle\dots

(354)

\displaystyle=\left\|{A_{q,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}\dots A_{1,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\mathsf{cpO}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{AI}}}A_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\Pi_{\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{I}}}\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}\mathsf{A}}}}\right\|+\sum_{t=0}^{q-1}O\left(\sqrt{\frac{s_{t}(\mathcal{R})}{N}}\right)

(355)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{q-1}O\left(\sqrt{\frac{s_{t}(\mathcal{R})}{N}}\right).

(356)

This, combined with
Lemma

7.1
implies the claim.
∎

One of the simplest examples from the literature of a predicate search problem is the double-sided zero search problem, due to Unruh
[
Unr21
,
Unr23
]
.

{problem}

Sample
\varphi\sim\mathbf{S}_{N}
for
N=2^{2n}
, to produce two way accessible oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
.
\mathcal{A}
makes
q
queries to
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
, and succeeds if it finds
(x\|0^{n},y\|0^{n})
such that
\varphi(x\|0^{n})=y\|0^{n})
.

This is a search problem with predicate
\mathcal{R}=\{[(x\|0^{n},y\|0^{n})]\,:\,x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}\}
. It is easy to compute
s_{t}(\mathcal{R})=O(2^{n})
for
t=o(2^{n})
, giving us the following bound.

Corollary 7.6

(of
Theorem

7.5
)
.

Any algorithm
\mathcal{A}
making
q
quantum queries solves
Section

7.2
with probability at most

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{2}}{2^{n}}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}\right).

(357)

Lower bounds for this problem are known already
[
CP24
,
MMW24
,
Coj+25
]
, even tight ones. Observe that our bound would be tight, ignoring that
{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}
term. The problems we will look at next are less well understood, and for many no tight bound is known. The bounds we show as applications of our general theorem will in some cases improve on known results and/or be tight.

Even more intriguingly, all of them would be tight if the
{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}
term were ignored. This suggests that our construction accurately captures the difficulty of these problems, and improving our soundness analysis provides a direct path towards showing tight quantum query lower bounds for many problems in the random permutation model.

7.3
Application to sponge

The sponge construction lifts an invertible permutation to a hash function with arbitrary domain. It is parameterized by integers
r,c\in\mathbb{N}
and the permutation
\varphi:\{0,1\}^{r+c}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{r+c}
. To hash a message
m\in(\{0,1\}^{r})^{*}
of the form
m=m_{1}\|m_{2}\|\dots\|m_{l}
, we initialize the internal state to
u_{0}=0^{r+c}
. Then, for every
i
from
1
up to
l
, we XOR
m_{i}
into the first
r
bits of
u
and apply
\varphi
, obtaining
u_{i+1}=\varphi(u_{i}\oplus(m_{i}\|0^{c}))
. The output is then the first
r
bits of the final state
u_{l}
.
12
12

12

For simplicity, we consider only one round of squeezing.
This construction is due to Bertoni et al
[
Ber+07
,
Ber+08
]
, and when instantiated with the Keccak permutation underlies the NIST standardized hash function SHA3
[
Ber+11a
,
Ber+11
]
.

It is known that this construction is quantum indifferentiable from a random oracle
[
Ala+25
]
, which in particular implies preimage and collision resistance. The best known bound for these is due to the same work, which shows
\tilde{\Omega}(2^{\min(r,c)/5})
queries are required for constant success probability at either collision or preimage finding. This result required a long and detailed proof tailored to this specific construction, and is not tight. Using our framework, we can improve these bounds in a direct manner.

We will use the notation
x[a:b]
to denote the
a
-th (inclusive) through
b
-th (exclusive) bits of string
x
with zero based indexing. Then, we can define the preimage predicate of the sponge for image
w\in\{0,1\}^{r}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{w-\mathsf{pre}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{[(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})]\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}[r:r+c]=0^{c},\text{ and }\\
y_{i}[r:r+c]=x_{i+1}[r:r+c],\text{ and }\\
y_{l}[0:r]=z.\end{subarray}\right\}

(358)

{problem}

[Sponge inversion.]
Given quantum query access to an ideal random permutation
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
, find a sponge pre-image of some fixed
w\in\{0,1\}^{r}
. Equivalently, solve the search problem defined by predicate
\mathcal{R}^{w-\mathsf{pre}}
.

Theorem 7.7
.

A
q
query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
solves sponge inversion,
Section

7.3
, with probability no more than

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}=O\left(\frac{q^{2}}{2^{r}}+\frac{q^{3}}{2^{c}}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}\right)

(359)

Proof.

To show this bound, we will change the predicate slightly. In particular, define the internal
z
-preimage predicate as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{z-\mathsf{ipre}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{[(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{l},y_{l})]\,:\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}[r:r+c]=0^{c},\text{ and}\\
y_{i}[r:r+c]=x_{i+1}[r:r+c],\text{ and }\\
y_{l}[r:r+c]=z.\end{subarray}\right\},

(360)

which can be used to construct the internal collision predicate

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{icol}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{R}^{0^{c}-\mathsf{ipre}}\cup\bigcup_{z\in\{0,1\}^{c}\setminus 0^{c}}\mathcal{R}^{z-\mathsf{ipre}}\times\mathcal{R}^{z-\mathsf{ipre}}.

(361)

We then let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{icol}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{w-\mathsf{pre}}
; in other words, we allow the adversary to find either a pre-image of
w
or an internal collision. Clearly, this task is no harder than finding a sponge preimage. It is known that
s_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})=O\left(2^{c}+t\cdot 2^{r}\right)

[
LM22
]
, from which the claim follows.
∎

We can also define the sponge collision predicate,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{col}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{w\in\{0,1\}^{r}}\mathcal{R}^{w-\mathsf{pre}}\times\mathcal{R}^{w-\mathsf{pre}}.

(362)

This gives rise to the following search task.

{problem}

[Sponge collision.]
Given quantum query access to a random permutation oracle
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}
, find a sponge collision. Equivalently, solve the search problem defined by predicate
\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{col}}
.

Theorem 7.8
.

A
q
query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
solves sponge collision,
Section

7.3
, with probability no more than

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}=O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{2^{\min(r,c)}}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}\right)

(363)

Proof.

To show this bound, we will change the predicate slightly. In particular, we once again allow the adversary to find either a sponge collision or an internal collision, letting
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{icol}}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\mathsf{col}}
. It is known that
s_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})=O\left(t\cdot 2^{r}+t\cdot 2^{c}\right)

[
Unr21
]
, from which the claim follows.
∎

Observe that using quantum collision and claw finding
[
BHT97
]
, there is an algorithm solving
Section

7.3
with probability
O(q^{3}2^{-\min(r,c)})
. It follows that
Theorem

7.8
is tight whenever
\min(r,c)/3\leq(r+c)/12
, and improves on the prior art
[
Ala+25
]
when
\min(r,c)/5\leq(r+c)/12
. Furthermore, ignoring the
{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}
term the bound would be tight for any
r
and
c
.

Similarly using Grover search
[
Gro96
]
or claw finding, there is an algorithm solving
Section

7.3
with probability
O(q^{3}/2^{r}+q^{2}/2^{c})
. Then
Theorem

7.7
is tight whenever
\min(r/2,c/3)\leq(r+c)/12
, and improves on the prior art
[
Ala+25
]
when
\min(r,c)/5\leq(r+c)/12
. Again, ignoring the
{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}
term the bound would be tight for any
r
and
c
.

7.4
Application to Davies-Meyer

Davies-Meyer is a construction that turns an ideal cipher into a fixed length compressing hash function
[
Win84
]
. It underlies SHA1, SHA2, and MD5. A cipher is a keyed permutation, and the quantum ideal cipher model allows quantum query access across both the key and the resulting permutation. We will state our results for the key-less case for simplicity.
13
13

13

Our framework can be applied to the ideal cipher model, though we leave a detailed exploration to future work.

The Davies-Meyer function
\mathsf{DM}:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
is defined by a permutation
\varphi:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, and is

\displaystyle\mathsf{DM}^{\varphi}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\varphi(x)\oplus x.

(364)

{problem}

[Davies-Meyer inversion.]
Given bidirectional quantum query access to a random permutation
\varphi:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, find an
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\mathsf{DM}^{\varphi}(x)=0^{n}
.

Theorem 7.9
.

Any
q
query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
solves Davies-Meyer inversion,
Section

7.4
, with probability bounded by

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{2}}{2^{n}}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}\right).

(365)

Proof.

We can define the zero preimage predicate
\mathcal{R}=\{[(x,x)]\,:\,x\in\{0,1\}^{n}\}
, which satisfies
s_{t}(\mathcal{R})=1
. Then the claim follows from
Theorem

7.5
.
∎

{problem}

[Davies-Meyer collision.]
Given bidirectional quantum query access to a random permutation
\varphi:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
, find a pair
x\neq x^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
\mathsf{DM}^{\varphi}(x)=\mathsf{DM}^{\varphi}(x^{\prime})
.

Theorem 7.10
.

Any
q
query quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
solves Davies-Meyer collision,
Section

7.4
, with probability bounded by

\displaystyle p_{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{q^{3}}{2^{n}}+{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}\right).

(366)

Proof.

We can define the collision predicate

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\left\{[(x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})]\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n},\text{ and }\\
x\oplus y=x^{\prime}\oplus y^{\prime},\text{ and }\\
x\neq x^{\prime},y\neq y^{\prime}.\end{subarray}\right\},

(367)

which satisfies
s_{t}(\mathcal{R})=t
. Then the claim follows from
Theorem

7.5
.
∎

Note that
[
HY18
,
Coj+25
]
prove bounds for preimage and collision resistance of the Davies-Meyer construction, and these bounds are tighter than the ones given here. However, no tight bound is known for collision resistance: the best known is
\Omega(2^{n/4})
for constant success probability due to
[
Coj+25
]
.

Ignoring the
{\mathsf{Dist}(q)}^{2}
term, our bound would be
\Omega(2^{n/3})
, matching quantum collision finding. Tightening our framework then provides a path towards deriving a tight collision bound for the Davies-Meyer construction.
